\displaystyle\alpha_{2}^{\tt m}(Q;B,d)\leq B\sqrt{\frac{1}{4(k_{g}-1)^{2}}+{\frac{a_{g}(h_{g}-1)}{4(k_{g}-1)^{2}}}+1}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{9+3\ln s}{(k-1)^{2}}+1},

\displaystyle\beta_{2}^{\tt m}(Q;B,d)\leq\frac{B^{2}}{M_{g,h_{g}-1}}.

Proof.

The worst-case second moment of A-RATQ:

By Theorem
5.2
we have

\displaystyle\alpha_{2}^{\tt m}(Q;B,d)

\displaystyle\leq\alpha_{2}^{\tt m}(Q_{\tt a};B,1)\cdot\alpha_{2}(Q_{{\tt at},R};1,d)

\displaystyle\leq\alpha_{2}^{\tt m}(Q_{\tt a};B,1)\cdot\sqrt{\frac{9+3\ln s}{(k-1)^{2}}+1}

\displaystyle\leq B\sqrt{\frac{1}{4(k_{g}-1)^{2}}+{\frac{a_{g}(h_{g}-1)}{4(k_{g}-1)^{2}}}+1}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{9+3\ln s}{(k-1)^{2}}+1},

where the second inequality used Theorem
4.2
with
B=1
,
and the third follows by Lemma
5.5
.

The worst-case bias of A-RATQ:

With parameters of RATQ set as in (
14
), we have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[Q_{{\tt at},R}(y)\right]=y,\quad\forall y\quad s.t\quad\|y\|_{2}^{2}\leq 1.

Therefore, by Theorem
5.2
it follows that

\displaystyle\beta_{2}^{\tt m}(Q;B,d)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{Y:\mathbb{E}\left[\|Y\|_{2}^{2}\right]\leq B^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\bigg|\mathbb{E}\left[Q_{\tt a}(\|Y\|_{2})-\|Y\|_{2}|Y\right]\bigg|\right]\leq\frac{B^{2}}{M_{g,h_{g}-1}},

where the second inequality follows from Lemma
5.5
.
∎

Note that RATQ yields an unbiased estimator; the bias in A-RATQ arises
from AGUQ since the gain is not bounded. Further, AGUQ uses a precision
of
\left\lceil\log h_{g}\right\rceil+\left\lceil\log(k_{g}+1)\right\rceil
bits, and therefore, the
overall precision of A-RATQ is
\left\lceil\log h_{g}\right\rceil+\left\lceil\log(k_{g}+1)\right\rceil+\left\lceil d/s\right\rceil\left\lceil\log h\right\rceil+d\left\lceil\log(k+1)\right\rceil
bits.

In the high-precision regime, we set

\displaystyle a_{g}

\displaystyle=2,\quad\log h_{g}=\left\lceil\log(1+\frac{1}{2}\log T)\right\rceil,

\displaystyle\log(k_{g}+1)

\displaystyle=\left\lceil\log\left(2+\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\log T+1}\right)\right\rceil.

(18)

Corollary 5.7
.

Denote by
Q
the quantizer A-RATQ
with parameters set as
in (
14
), (
10
),
and (
18
). Then,
the overall precision
r
used by
Q

is less than

d(1+\Delta_{1})+\Delta_{2}+\left\lceil\log\bigg(2+\sqrt{\log T+1}\bigg)\right\rceil,

where
\Delta_{1}=\left\lceil\log\left(2+\sqrt{9+3\ln\Delta_{2}}\right)\right\rceil
and

\Delta_{2}=\left\lceil\log(1+\ln^{*}({d}/{3}))\right\rceil
, the same as
Corollary
4.3
.
Furthermore, the optimization protocol
\pi

given in algorithm
1
satisfies

\sup_{(f,O)\in\mathcal{O}}\mathcal{E}(f,O,\pi,Q)\leq{3DB}/{\sqrt{T}}
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to the proof of Corollary
4.3
. The first statement follows by simply upper bounding the precision of the fixed-length code for A-RATQ with parameters as in the statement. The second statement follows by bounding

\sup_{(f,O)\in\mathcal{O}}\mathcal{E}(f,O,\pi,Q)
using Theorem
3.2
, using the upper bounds for
\alpha
and
\beta
given in Theorem
5.6
, and finally substituting the parameters.
∎

Remark 19
.

The precision used in Corollary
5.7

matches the lower bound in Corollary
4.1
upto an additive
factor of
\log\log T
(ignoring the mild factor of
\log\log\log\ln^{*}(d/3)
), which is much lower than the
\log T
lower bound we established for uniform
gain quantizers. Hence, the precision requirement of A-RATQ in the high-precision regime is considerably smaller than the precision requirement of uniform gain quantizers established in Theorem
5.3
for
\log T\gg d(1+\Delta_{1})
, while remaining roughly the same for
\log T=O(d(1+\Delta_{1}))
.

3
A-RATQ in the low-precision regime

In order to operate with a fixed precision
r
, we combine
A-RATQ with RCS.
We simply combine RCS with RATQ as
in Section
3
to limit the precision and use AGUQ as the gain quantizer. Note that we use independent
randomness in our gain quantizer
Q_{a}(\|Y\|_{2})
and our shape quantizer
\tilde{Q}(Y/\|Y\|_{2})
, rendering them conditionally independent given
Y
. We have the following theorem characterizing
\alpha
and
\beta
for this quantizer.

Theorem 5.8
.

Let
Q(Y)=Q_{a}(\|Y\|)\cdot\tilde{Q}(Y/\|Y\|_{2})
, where
\tilde{Q}
is the composition of RCS and RATQ described in Theorem
5.3
with parameters
m
,
m_{0}
, and
h
of RATQ as in (
14
) and
Q_{a}
is AGUQ. Then,

\displaystyle\alpha_{2}^{\tt m}(Q;B,d)

\displaystyle\leq B\sqrt{\frac{1}{4(k_{g}-1)^{2}}+{\frac{a_{g}(h_{g}-1)}{4(k_{g}-1)^{2}}}+1}\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mu}}\sqrt{\frac{9+3\ln s}{(k-1)^{2}}+1},

\displaystyle\beta_{2}^{\tt m}(Q;B,d)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{B^{2}}{M_{g,h_{g}-1}}.

Proof.

The worst-case second moment:

Starting by applying Theorem
5.2
, we have

\displaystyle\alpha_{2}^{\tt m}(Q;B,d)

\displaystyle\leq\alpha_{2}^{\tt m}(Q_{\tt a};B,1)\cdot\alpha_{2}(\tilde{Q};1,d)

\displaystyle\leq\alpha_{2}^{\tt m}(Q_{\tt a};B,1)\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mu}}\alpha_{2}(Q_{{\tt at},R};1,d)

\displaystyle\leq B\sqrt{\frac{1}{4(k_{g}-1)^{2}}+{\frac{a_{g}(h_{g}-1)}{4(k_{g}-1)^{2}}}+1}\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mu}}\sqrt{\frac{9+3\ln s}{(k-1)^{2}}+1},

where the second inequality follows by Theorem
5.3
and the third follows by Theorem
4.2
and Lemma
5.5
.

The worst-case bias:

With parameters of RATQ set as in (
14
), we have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\tilde{Q}(y)\right]=y,\quad\forall y\quad s.t\quad\|y\|_{2}^{2}\leq 1.

Therefore, by Theorem
5.2
we get

\displaystyle\beta_{2}^{\tt m}(Q;B,d)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{Y:\mathbb{E}\left[\|Y\|_{2}^{2}\right]\leq B^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\bigg|\mathbb{E}\left[Q_{\tt a}(\|Y\|_{2})-\|Y\|_{2}|Y\right]\bigg|\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{B^{2}}{M_{g,h_{g}-1}},

where the second inequality follows from Lemma
5.5
.

∎

We divide the total precision
r
into
r_{g}
and
r_{s}
bits:
r_{g}
to quantize the gain,
r_{s}
to quantize the subsampled shape vector.
We set

\displaystyle s,k,\text{~and~}\mu d\text{~as in \eqref{e:RATQ_RCS_params}, with $r_{s}$ replacing $r$,}

\displaystyle{\log h_{g}}=\log(k_{g}+1)={\frac{r_{g}}{2}},~a_{g}=\left(\mu T\right)^{\frac{1}{h_{g}+1}}

(19)

That is, our shape quantizer simply quantizes
\mu d
randomly chosen
coordinates of the rotated vector using ATUQ with
r_{s}
bits, and the
remaining bits are used by the gain quantizer AGUQ.
The result below shows the performance of this quantizer.

Corollary 5.9
.

For any
r
with gain quantizer being assigned
r_{g}\geq 4
bits and shape quantizer being assigned
r_{s}\geq 3+\left\lceil\log(1+\ln^{*}({d}/{3}))\right\rceil
, let
Q

be the combination of RCS and A-RATQ with parameters set as
in (
14
), (
17
), (
19
). Then
for
\mu T\geq 1
, the optimization protocol
\pi
in
Algorithm
1

can obtain

\sup_{(f,O)\in\mathcal{O}}\mathcal{E}(f,O,\pi,Q)\leq O\left(DB\left(\frac{d}{T\min\{d,\frac{r_{s}}{\log\ln^{*}(d/3)}\}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{2^{r_{g}/2}-1}{2^{r_{g}/2}+1}}\right).

Proof.

By using Theorem
3.2
to upper bound
\sup_{(f,O)\in\mathcal{O}_{{\tt c},2}^{\tt m}}\mathcal{E}(f,O,\pi,Q)
and then Theorem
5.8
to upper-bound
\alpha
and
\beta
, we get

\displaystyle\sup_{(f,O)\in\mathcal{O}_{{\tt c},2}^{\tt m}}\mathcal{E}(f,O,\pi,Q)

\displaystyle\leq D\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mu T}}\sqrt{\frac{B^{2}}{4(k_{g}-1)^{2}}+\frac{a_{g}(h_{g}-1)B^{2}}{4(k_{g}-1)^{2}}+B^{2}}\sqrt{\frac{9+3\ln s}{(k-1)^{2}}+1}+\frac{B^{2}}{M_{g,h-1}}\right).

By substituting the parameters as in the statement and using the fact that
\mu T\geq 1
completes the proof.
∎

Remark 20
.

Our fixed precision quantizer in
Corollary
5.9
establishes that using only a constant number of bits for gain-quantization, we get very close to the lower bound in Theorem
4.4
. For instance, given access to a large enough precision
r
, if we set
r_{g}
to be
16
bits, we get

\sup_{(f,O)\in\mathcal{O}}\mathcal{E}(f,O,\pi,Q)\leq O\left(DB\left(\frac{d}{T\min\{d,\frac{r-16}{\log\ln^{*}(d/3)}\}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{255}{257}}\right).

Here, the ratio of
d/(\min\{d,\frac{r-16}{\log\ln^{*}(d/3)}\})
is very close to the optimal ratio of
d/(\min\{d,r\})
, and the exponent
255/(2\cdot 257)
is close to the optimal exponent
1/2
.

Remark 21
.

We remark that A-RATQ satisfies Assumptions (1) and (2) in Section
1
but not (3), and breaches the lower bound for uniform gain quantizers established in Section
1
.

4
A variable-length quantizer

So far in this thesis, we have restricted our quantizers to be fixed-length. We now present a variable-length quantizer for mean square bounded oracles which improves over the convergence rate of Corollary
5.9
. The quantizer we present is a gain-shape quantizer that uses RATQ as the shape quantizer but uses a variable-length version of the gain quantizer called AGUQ
+
, an update on AGUQ presented in the previous section.

AGUQ
+
differs from AGUQ in two crucial aspects: 1)
The number of uniform levels of the uniform quantizer for different dynamic ranges is different. Denote by
k_{g,j}
the number of uniform levels corresponding to the range
[0,M_{g,j}]
. We choose
k_{g,j}
to grow geometrically.
2) AGUQ
+
employs entropic compression further to reduce the expected code-length of the quantized representation.
Besides the differences,
the quantization in AGUQ
+
is the same as that of AGUQ.

Specifically, AGUQ
+
is once again an adaptive quantizer like AGUQ with dynamic ranges growing in a goemetric manner, precisely in the same way as in (
17
).
For a given gain
G\geq 0
, AGUQ
+
first identifies the
smallest
j
such that
G\leq M_{g,j}
and then represents
G
using the one-dimensional version of CUQ with a dynamic range
[0,M_{g,j}]
and

k_{g,j}
uniform levels

\displaystyle B_{M_{g,j}}(\ell):=\ell\cdot\frac{M_{g,j}}{k_{g,j}-1},\quad\forall\,\ell\in\{0,\ldots,k_{g,j}-1\}.

As in AGUQ, if
G>M_{g,h_{g}-1}
,
the overflow
\emptyset
symbol is used and the decoder simply outputs

0
.

Note that since we are quantizing unbounded random variables, it is difficult to avoid bias. Nevertheless, we will make the effect of the bias negligible. Specifically, for the application of communication-constrained optimization of convex functions, it will be desirable to have a bias of at the most
1/\sqrt{T}
. To achieve this, we set

\displaystyle a_{g}=2,\quad h_{g}=1+\frac{1}{2}\log T,\quad k_{g,j}+1=2^{j+1}.

(20)

We now describe the variable length coding procedure. The variable-length bit string itself is a concatenation of two separate bit strings. The first string represents the non-uniform level
j
in
\{0,\cdots,h_{g}-1\}
using the first
h_{g}
symbols of the Huffman code for geometric distribution with parameter
1/2
. The second string uses a fixed-length code of
k_{g,j}
bits. We will show that the total number of bits used for both the strings would be
O(1)
in expectation.

Remark 22

(Entropic compression in
AGUQ^{+}
)
.

AGUQ^{+}
improves over vanilla
AGUQ
by exploiting that the probability of a larger dynamic range being chosen decays exponentially with the dynamic range level
j
. For concreteness, since
\mathbb{E}\left[Y^{2}\right]\leq B^{2},
we have by Markov’s inequality

P(|Y|>M_{g,j-1})\leq B^{2}/M^{2}_{g,j-1}=a_{g}^{-j}=2^{-j+1}.
This probability bound allows us to use a code similar to that of Huffman code for geometric distribution and only have a constant code-length.

The following result characterizes the performance of one-dimensional quantizer AGUQ
+
.

Lemma 5.10
.

Let
Q_{{\tt a^{+}}}
be
the quantizer AGUQ
+
described above with parameters set as in (
17
) and (
20
). Then, for

Y
such that
\mathbb{E}\left[Y^{2}\right]\leq B^{2},

Q_{\tt a^{+}}(Y)
can be represented using at the most
O(1)
bits of precision in expectation,

\displaystyle\alpha_{2}^{\tt m}(Q_{\tt a^{+}};B,1)\leq O\left(1\right),\text{ and }

\displaystyle\beta_{2}^{\tt m}(Q_{{\tt a^{+}}};B,1)\leq\sup_{Y\geq 0\text{ a.s. }:\mathbb{E}\left[Y^{2}\right]\leq B^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\bigg|\mathbb{E}\left[Q_{a}(Y)-Y|Y\right]\bigg|\right]\leq O\left(\frac{B}{\sqrt{T}}\right).

The proof is deferred to Section
5

Remark 23
.

Thus employing a gain-shape quantizer where the gain is quantized by AGUQ
+

and the shape is quantized by the subsampled version of RATQ along with PSGD improves over the convergence guarantees of Corollary
5.9
, and essentially has the same order of convergence guarantees as that in Corollary
4.5
. That is, this particular gain-shape quantizer along with PSGD achieves roughly the convergence rate of
O\left(\frac{DB}{\sqrt{T}}\cdot\frac{d}{r}\right)
, which is the same as that in lower-bound for communication constrained optimization of convex and
\ell_{2}
lipschitz functions,
\mathcal{O}_{{\tt c},2}^{m}
, as stated in Theorem
4.4
. From Remark
7
, the lower bound in Theorem
4.4
holds for variable length quantizers, too, provided the gradient processing is done in a nonadaptive manner. Thus employing a gain-shape quantizer where the gain is quantized by AGUQ
+

and the shape is quantized by the subsampled version of RATQ along with PSGD is optimal for communication-constrained optimization of
\mathcal{O}_{{\tt c},2}^{m}
when nonadaptive, variable-length quantizers are allowed.

6
Main proofs

1
Proof of Theorem
3.2

We proceed as in the standard proof of convergence (see, for
instance,
[
15
]
): Denoting by
\Gamma_{\mathcal{X}}(x)
the
projection of
x
on the set
\mathcal{X}
(in the Euclidean norm), the error
at time
t
can be bounded as

\displaystyle\|x_{t}-x^{*}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\|\Gamma_{\mathcal{X}}\big(x_{t-1}-\eta Q(\hat{g}(x_{t-1}))\big)-x^{*}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|\big(x_{t-1}-\eta Q(\hat{g}(x_{t-1}))\big)-x^{*}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\|x_{t-1}-x^{*}\|_{2}^{2}+\|\eta Q(\hat{g}(x_{t-1}))\|_{2}^{2}-2\eta(x_{t-1}-x^{*})^{T}Q(\hat{g}(x_{t-1}))

\displaystyle=\|x_{t-1}-x^{*}\|_{2}^{2}+\|\eta Q(\hat{g}(x_{t-1}))\|_{2}^{2}-2\eta(x_{t-1}-x^{*})^{T}\big(Q(\hat{g}(x_{t-1}))-\hat{g}(x_{t-1})\big)

\displaystyle\quad-2\eta(x_{t-1}-x^{*})^{T}\hat{g}(x_{t-1}),

where the first inequality is
a well known property of the projection operator
\Gamma
(see,
for instance, Lemma 3.1,

[
15
]
). By rearranging the terms, we have

\displaystyle 2\eta(x_{t-1}-x^{*})^{T}\hat{g}(x_{t-1})

\displaystyle\leq\|x_{t-1}-x^{*}\|_{2}^{2}-\|x_{t}-x^{*}\|_{2}^{2}+\|\eta Q(\hat{g}(x_{t-1}))\|_{2}^{2}

(21)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt-2\eta(x_{t-1}-x^{*})^{T}\left(Q(\hat{g}(x_{t-1}))-\hat{g}(x_{t-1})\right).

(22)

Also, since
\mathbb{E}\left[\hat{g}(x_{t-1})|x_{t-1}\right]
is a subgradient at
x_{t-1}
for the convex function
f
,
upon taking expectation over the randomness in the subgradient estimates as well as the quantizer output we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[f(x_{t-1})-f(x^{*})\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[(x_{t-1}-x^{*})^{T}\mathbb{E}\left[\hat{g}(x_{t-1})|x_{t-1}\right]\right],

(23)

which with the previous bound yields

\displaystyle 2\eta\mathbb{E}\left[f(x_{t-1})-f(x^{*})\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\|x_{t-1}-x^{*}\|_{2}^{2}\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[\|x_{t}-x^{*}\|_{2}^{2}\right]+\eta^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\|Q(\hat{g}(x_{t-1}))\|_{2}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt-2\eta\mathbb{E}\left[(x_{t-1}-x^{*})^{T}\big(Q(\hat{g}(x_{t-1}))-\hat{g}(x_{t-1})\big)\right].

Next, by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality
and the assumption in (1), the third term on the right-side above
can be bounded further to obtain

\displaystyle 2\eta\mathbb{E}\left[f(x_{t-1})-f(x^{*})\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\|x_{t-1}-x^{*}\|_{2}^{2}\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[\|x_{t}-x^{*}\|_{2}^{2}\right]+\eta^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\|Q(\hat{g}(x_{t-1}))\|_{2}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+2\eta\cdot D\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[\|\mathbb{E}\left[Q(\hat{g}(x_{t-1}))-\hat{g}(x_{t-1})|x_{t-1}\right]\|_{2}\right].

Finally, we note that, by
the definition of
\alpha
and
\beta
, for
L_{2}
-bounded
oracles we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\|Q(\hat{g}(x_{t-1}))\|_{2}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\alpha_{2}^{\tt m}(Q)^{2},

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{E}\left[Q(\hat{g}(x_{t-1}))-\hat{g}(x_{t-1})|x_{t-1}\right]\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\beta_{2}^{\tt m}(Q),

which gives

\displaystyle 2\eta\mathbb{E}\left[f(x_{t-1})-f(x^{*})\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\|x_{t-1}-x^{*}\|_{2}^{2}\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[\|x_{t}-x^{*}\|_{2}^{2}\right]+\eta^{2}\alpha_{2}^{\tt m}(Q)^{2}+2\eta D\beta_{2}^{\tt m}(Q).

Therefore, by summing from
t=2
to
T+1
, dividing by
T
, and using
assumption that the domain
\mathcal{X}
has diameter at the most
D
, we have

\displaystyle 2\eta\mathbb{E}\left[f(\bar{x}_{T})-f(x^{*})\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{D^{2}}{T}+\eta^{2}\alpha_{2}^{\tt m}(Q)^{2}+2\eta D\beta_{2}^{\tt m}(Q).

The first statement of Theorem
3.2
follows upon
dividing by
\eta
and setting the value of
\eta
as in the
statement. The second statement holds in a similar manner by replacing

\alpha
and
\beta
with
\alpha_{2}
and
\beta_{2}
, respectively.
∎

2
Proof of Theorem
3.3

Note that since the gradient descent step remains the same, (
21
) still holds. Except now, instead of (
23
), we have a stronger inequality

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[f(x_{t-1})-f(x^{*})\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[(x_{t-1}-x^{*})^{T}\mathbb{E}\left[\hat{g}(x_{t-1})|x_{t-1}\right]\right]-\frac{\gamma}{2}\|x_{t-1}-x^{*}\|_{2}^{2},

(24)

Combining (
21
) and (
24
), and then using the definition of
\alpha
,
\beta
as in the previous proof, we get

\displaystyle 2\eta_{t-1}\mathbb{E}\left[f(x_{t-1})-f(x^{*})\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\|x_{t-1}-x^{*}\|_{2}^{2}\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[\|x_{t}-x^{*}\|_{2}^{2}\right]+\eta^{2}_{t-1}\alpha_{2}(Q)^{2}+2\eta_{t-1}D\beta_{2}(Q)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt-\frac{\gamma}{2}\|x_{t-1}-x^{*}\|_{2}^{2}.

Then multiplying by
(t-1)/2\eta_{t-1}
, we get

\displaystyle(t-1)\mathbb{E}\left[f(x_{t-1})-f(x^{*})\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{(t-1)\eta_{t-1}}{2}\alpha_{2}(Q)^{2}+\left(\frac{t-1}{2\eta_{t-1}}-\frac{(t-1)\gamma}{2}\right)\mathbb{E}\left[\|x_{t-1}-x^{*}\|_{2}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt-\frac{t-1}{2\eta_{t-1}}\mathbb{E}\left[\|x_{t}-x^{*}\|_{2}^{2}\right]+(t-1)D\beta_{2}(Q).

Substituting for
\eta_{t-1}
as in the theorem statement, summing the resultant inequality from
t=1
to
T
, and then applying Jensen’s inequality completes the proof.

3
Proof of Theorem
4.2

Step 1: Analysis of CUQ.

We first prove a result for CUQ (with a dynamic range of
[-M,M]
)
which will bound the expected value of

\sum_{i\in[d]}\big(Q_{{\tt u}}(Y)(i)-{Y(i)}\big)^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{\{|{Y(i)|}\leq M\}},

namely the mean square error when there is no overflow. This will be
useful in the analysis of RATQ, too.

Lemma 6.1
.

For an
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-valued random variable
Y
and
Q_{{\tt u}}
denoting the quantizer CUQ
with parameters
M
(with dynamic range
[-M,M]
)
and
k
, let
Q_{{\tt u}}(Y)
be the quantized value of
Y
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i\in[d]}\big(Q_{{\tt u}}(Y)(i)-{Y(i)}\big)^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{\{{|Y(i)|}\leq M\}}\mid Y\right]\leq\frac{dM^{2}}{(k-1)^{2}}\left(\frac{1}{d}\sum_{j\in[d]}\mathbbm{1}_{\{{|Y(j)|}\leq M\}}\right).

The proof is relatively straightforward with the
calculations similar to
[
88
, Theorem 2]
; it is
deferred to Section
1
.

Also, the quantizer AGUQ in Section
2
uses the
one-dimensional CUQ with dynamic range
[0,M]
as a subroutine. The
uniform levels for this variant of CUQ are given by

B_{M,k}(\ell)=\ell\cdot\frac{M}{k-1},\forall\ell\in[k-1].

We have the following lemma for this variant of CUQ.

Lemma 6.2
.

For an
\mathbb{R}
-valued random variable
Y
which is almost surely nonnegative
and the quantizer
Q_{\tt u}
with dynamic range
[0,M]
and parameter
k
, let
Q_{{\tt u}}(Y)

denote the quantized value of
Y
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\big(Q_{{\tt u}}(Y)-{Y}\big)^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{\{{|Y|}\leq M\}}\mid Y\right]\leq\frac{M^{2}}{4(k-1)^{2}}\left(\mathbbm{1}_{\{{|Y|}\leq M\}}\right).

The proof is very similar to the proof of Lemma
6.1
and is deferred to Section
1
.

Step 2: Mean square error for adaptive quantizers.

The quantizers RATQ and A-RATQ use ATUQ as subroutine; in addition, A-RATQ uses AGUQ for gain quantization. Thus, in order to analyze RATQ and A-RATQ, we need to analyze
ATUQ and AGUQ first.

In this step we provide a general bound on the mean square error of adaptive quantizers. We capture the performances of ATUQ and AGUQ in two separate results below.

Lemma 6.3
.

For an
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-valued random variable
Y
and
Q
denoting the quantizer ATUQ with dynamic-range
parameters
M_{j}
s, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i\in[d]}\big(Q(Y)(i)-{Y(i)}\big)^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{\{{|Y(i)|}\leq M_{h-1}\}}\right]\leq\frac{d}{(k-1)^{2}}\left(m+m_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{h-1}M_{j}^{2}P\left(\|Y\|_{\infty}>M_{j-1}\right)\right).

Proof.

Consider the events
A_{j}
s corresponding to different levels used by
the adaptive quantizer of the norm, defined as follows:

\displaystyle A_{0}

\displaystyle:=\{\|Y\|_{\infty}\leq m\},

\displaystyle A_{j}

\displaystyle:=\{M_{j-1}<\|Y\|_{\infty}\leq M_{j}\},\quad\forall j\in[h-2],

\displaystyle A_{h-1}

\displaystyle:=\{M_{h-2}<\|Y\|_{\infty}\}.

By construction,
\sum_{j=0}^{h-1}\mathbbm{1}_{A_{j}}=1\text{ a.s.}
. Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i\in[d]}\big(Q(Y)(i)-{Y(i)}\big)^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{\{{|Y(i)|}\leq M_{h-1}\}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\|Q(Y)-{Y}\|_{2}^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{A_{0}}\right]+\sum_{j=1}^{h-2}\mathbb{E}\left[\|Q(Y)-{Y}\|_{2}^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{A_{j}}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i\in[d]}\big(Q_{{\tt u}}(Y)(i)-{Y(i)}\big)^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{\{{|Y(i)|}\leq M_{h-1}\}}\mathbbm{1}_{A_{h-1}}\right].

Note that
\mathbbm{1}_{A_{0}}
implies
that we are using a
k
-level uniform quantization with a dynamic
range of
[-m,m]
. Therefore, this term can be bounded by
Lemma
6.1
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\|Q(Y)-{Y}\|_{2}^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{A_{0}}\right]\ \leq\frac{dm}{(k-1)^{2}}.

Under the event
A_{j}
with
j\in[h-1]
, we use a
k
-level uniform
quantization with a dynamic range of
[-M_{j},M_{j}].
Therefore, by
Lemma
6.1
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\|Q(Y)-{Y}\|_{2}^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{A_{j}}\right]\leq

\displaystyle\frac{dM_{j}^{2}}{(k-1)^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{A_{j}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{dM_{j}^{2}}{(k-1)^{2}}P\left(\|{Y}\|_{\infty}>M_{j-1}\right).

∎

Note that the proof above does note use specific form of
M_{j}
’s and therefore applies as it is for the one-dimensional AGUQ gain quantizer used in A-RATQ; the only change is the fact that instead of using Lemma
6.1
for uniform quantization we use Lemma
6.2
. This leads to the following lemma, which will be useful later in the analysis of A-RATQ.

Lemma 6.4
.

For an
\mathbb{R}
-valued random variable
Y
which is almost surely nonnegative
and
Q
denoting the quantizer AGUQ with dynamic-range
parameters
M_{g,j}
s, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[(Q(Y)-{Y})^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{\{|Y|\leq M_{g,h-1}\}}\right]\leq\frac{1}{4(k-1)^{2}}\left(B^{2}+\sum_{j=1}^{h-1}M_{g,j}^{2}P\left(|Y|>M_{g,j-1}\right)\right).

The proof is similar to that of Lemma
6.3
and is omitted.

Step 3: Mean square error of ATUQ for a subgaussian input vector.

In our analysis, we need to evaluate the performance of ATUQ for
subgaussian
input vectors.

Definition 6.5

(
cf.

[
13
]
)
.

A centered random variable
X
is said to be subgaussian with variance factor
v
if for all
\lambda
in
\mathbb{R}
, we have

\ln\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\lambda X}\right]\leq\frac{\lambda^{2}v}{2}.

The following well-known fact (
cf.

[
13
, Chapter 2]
) will be used throughout.

Lemma 6.6
.

For a centered subgaussian random variable
X
with variance factor
v
the

\displaystyle P(|X|>x)\leq 2e^{-x^{2}/2v},\quad\forall\,x>0,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[X^{2}\right]\leq 4v,\quad\mathbb{E}\left[X^{4}\right]\leq 32v^{2}.

Next, consider the quantizer
Q_{{\tt at},I}
which is similar to RATQ but skips the rotation step. Specifically,
Q_{{\tt at},I}
is obtained by replacing the random matrix
R
in the encoder and decoder of RATQ (given in Algorithms
2
and
3
, respectively) by the identity matrix
I
. Symbolically, the quantizer
Q_{{\tt at},I}
can be described as follows for the
d
-dimensional input vector
Y

Q_{{\tt at},I}(Y)=[Q_{\tt at}(Y_{1})^{T},\cdots,Q_{\tt at}(Y_{\left\lceil d/s\right\rceil})^{T}],

(25)

where
Q_{\tt at}
is the quantizer ATUQ and
Y_{i}
is the
i^{th}
subvector of
Y
. Recall that the
i^{th}
subvector
Y_{i}
comprises
the coordinates
\{(i-1)s+1,\cdots,\min\{is,d\}\}
,
for all
i\in[d/s].
Also, recall that the dimension of all the sub
vectors except the last one is
s
, with the last one having
dimension
d-s\lfloor d/s\rfloor
.

Notice that like RATQ,
Q_{at,I}
has parameters
k
,
h
,
s
,
m
, and
m_{0}
which need to be set.
We set the parameters
m
and
m_{0}
to be
3v
and
2v\ln s
,
respectively, and prove a general lemma in terms of the other
parameters of
Q_{at,I}
for a subgaussian input vector.

Lemma 6.7
.

Consider
Y=[Y(1),\ldots,Y(d))]^{T},
where for all
i
in
[d]
,

Y(i)
is a centered subgaussian random variable with variance factor

v
. Let
Q
denote the quantizer
Q_{{\tt at},I}
with parameters

m
and
m_{0}
set to
3v
and
2v\ln s
, respectively.
Then, for every
s,k,h\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

\frac{1}{d}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i\in[d]}(Y(i)-Q(Y)(i))^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{\{|Y(i)|\leq M_{h-1}}\}\right]\leq v\cdot\frac{9+3\ln s}{(k-1)^{2}}.

Proof.

Since

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i\in[d]}(Y(i)-Q(Y)(i))^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{\{|Y(i)|\leq M_{h-1}}\}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{\left\lceil\frac{d}{s}\right\rceil}\sum_{j=(i-1)s+1}^{\min\{is,d\}}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(Q_{{\tt at}}(Y)(j)-Y(j)\right)^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{\{|Y(j)|\leq M_{h-1}\}}\right],

by using Lemma
6.3
for each of the
\left\lceil d/s\right\rceil

subvectors, we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i\in[d]}(Y(i)-Q(Y)(i))^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{\{|Y(i)|\leq M_{h-1}}\}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{s}{(k-1)^{2}}\sum_{i\in 1}^{\lfloor\frac{d}{s}\rfloor}\left(m+m_{0}+\sum_{j\in[h-1]}M_{j}^{2}P\left(\|Y_{i,s}\|_{\infty}>M_{j-1}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 8.5359pt+\frac{(d-s\lfloor\frac{d}{s}\rfloor)}{(k-1)^{2}}\left(m+m_{0}+\sum_{j\in[h-1]}M_{j}^{2}P\left(\|Y_{\left\lceil d/s\right\rceil,s}\|_{\infty}>M_{j-1}\right)\right).

For all
i\in\lfloor d/s\rfloor
, it follows from the union bound that

\displaystyle P\left(\|Y_{1,s}\|_{\infty}>M_{j-1}\right)\leq 2se^{\frac{-M_{j-1}^{2}}{2v}}.

Also, since
d-s\lfloor d/s\rfloor\leq s
, we have

\displaystyle P\left(\|Y_{\left\lceil d/s\right\rceil,s}\|_{\infty}>M_{j-1}\right)\leq 2se^{\frac{-M_{j-1}^{2}}{2v}}.

Using these tail bounds in the previous inequality, we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i\in[d]}(Y(i)-Q(Y)(i))^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{\{|Y(i)|\leq M_{h-1}}\}\right]\leq\frac{d}{(k-1)^{2}}\left(m+m_{0}+2s\sum_{j\in[h-1]}M_{j}^{2}e^{\frac{-M_{j-1}^{2}}{2v}}\right).

Setting
m=3v
and
m_{0}=2v
, the summation
on the right-side is bounded further as

\displaystyle 2s\left(\frac{3v}{s}\sum_{j=1}^{h-1}(e^{*j})\cdot e^{-1.5{e^{*(j-1)}}}\right)+2s\left(\frac{2v}{s}\sum_{j=1}^{h-1}e^{-1.5{e^{*(j-1)}}}\right)

\displaystyle=6v\sum_{j=1}^{h-1}e^{-0.5{e^{*(j-1)}}}+4v\ln s\sum_{j=1}^{h-1}e^{-1.5{e^{*(j-1)}}}

\displaystyle\leq 6v\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}e^{-0.5{e^{*(j-1)}}}+4v\ln s\sum_{j=1}^{h-1}e^{-1.5{e^{*(j-1)}}}

\displaystyle\leq 6v+v\ln s,

where we use a bound of
1
for
\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}e^{-0.5{e^{*(j-1)}}}
, whose validity
can be seen as follows
11
11

11

In fact, these bounds motivate the use of tetration
as our choice for
M_{j}
s.

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}e^{-0.5{e^{*(j-1)}}}

\displaystyle={e^{-0.5}}+{e^{-0.5e}}+{e^{-0.5e^{e}}}+\sum_{j=3}^{\infty}e^{-0.5{e^{*(j)}}}

\displaystyle\leq{e^{-0.5}}+{e^{-0.5e}}+{e^{-0.5e^{e}}}+\sum_{j=3}^{\infty}e^{-0.5{je^{e}}}

\displaystyle\leq{e^{-0.5}}+{e^{-0.5e}}+{e^{-0.5e^{e}}}+\frac{1}{e^{e^{e}}-1}

\displaystyle\leq 1,

and
1/4
for
\sum_{j=1}^{h-1}e^{-1.5{e^{*(j-1)}}}
, whose validity can be seen as follows

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}e^{-1.5{e^{*(j-1)}}}

\displaystyle={e^{-1.5}}+{e^{-1.5e}}+{e^{-1.5e^{e}}}+\sum_{j=3}^{\infty}e^{-1.5{e^{*(j)}}}

\displaystyle\leq{e^{-1.5}}+{e^{-1.5e}}+{e^{-1.5e^{e}}}+\sum_{j=3}^{\infty}e^{-1.5{je^{e}}}

\displaystyle\leq{e^{-1.5}}+{e^{-1.5e}}+{e^{-1.5e^{e}}}+\frac{1}{e^{3e^{e}}-1/e^{1.5e^{e}}}

\displaystyle\leq 0.2401.

Therefore, we obtain

\frac{1}{d}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i\in[d]}(Y(i)-Q(Y)(i))^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{|Y(i)|\leq M_{h-1}}\right]\leq v\cdot\frac{9+3\ln s}{(k-1)^{2}}.

∎

We remark that calculations present in this lemma are at the heart of the analysis of RATQ.
Also, this lemma will be useful for other applications discussed in Chapters
4
and
5
.

Step 4: Completing the proof.

Recall that the random matrix
R
defined in (
6
) is used at the encoder of RATQ to randomly rotate the input vector. We observe that the rotated vector has subgaussian entries.

Lemma 6.8
.

For an
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-valued random variable
Y
such that
\|Y\|_{2}^{2}\leq B^{2}\text{ a.s.}
, all
coordinates of the rotated vector
RY
are centered subgaussian random
variables with a variance factor of
{B^{2}}/{d}
, whereby

P(|{RY}(j)|\geq M)\leq 2e^{-dM^{2}/2B^{2}},\quad\forall\,j\in[d],

where
RY(j)
is the
j^{th}
coordinate of the rotated vector.

The proof uses similar calculations as
[
7
]
and
[
88
]
; it is deferred to Section
2
.

Intuitively, the Lemma
6.8
highlights the fact that overall energy
\|Y\|_{2}^{2}
in the input vector
Y
is divided equally among all the coordinates after random rotation.

The worst-case second moment of RATQ.

Note that by the description of RATQ which will be denoted by
Q_{{\tt at},R}(RY),
we have that

Q_{{\tt at},R}(Y)=R^{-1}Q_{{\tt at},I}(RY),

where
Q_{{\tt at},I}
is as defined in (
25
). Thus,

\displaystyle Q_{{\tt at},I}(RY)=[Q_{{\tt at}}(RY_{1,s})^{T},\cdots,Q_{{\tt at}}(RY_{\left\lceil d/s\right\rceil,s})^{T}]^{T},

(26)

where
the subvector
RY_{i,s}
is given by

\displaystyle RY_{i,s}=[RY((i-1)s+1),\cdots,RY(\min\{is,d\})]^{T}.

To compute
\alpha(Q_{{\tt at},R}(Y))
, we will first compute the
second moment for the output of RATQ. Specifically, using the fact
R

is a unitary transform, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\|Q_{{\tt at},R}(Y)\|_{2}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\|R^{-1}Q_{{\tt at},I}(RY)\|_{2}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\|Q_{{\tt at},I}(RY)\|_{2}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in[d]}\mathbb{E}\left[(Q_{{\tt at},I}(RY)(j))^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{\left\lceil\frac{d}{s}\right\rceil}\sum_{j=(i-1)s+1}^{\min\{is,d\}}\mathbb{E}\left[(Q_{{\tt at},I}(RY)(j))^{2}\right].

We now observe that for our choice of
m
and
h
for RATQ given by (
8
), we have

M_{h-1}^{2}\geq m(e^{*{\log_{e}^{*}(d/3)}})=(3B^{2}/d).(d/3)=B^{2}.

Using this observation and noting that
R
is a unitary matrix,
we have that

\mathbbm{1}_{\{\|RY\|_{2}\leq M_{h-1}\}}=1\text{ a.s.}.

Also, noting that
|RY(j)|\leq\|RY\|_{2}=\|Y\|_{2}=B
a.s., for all
j\in[d]
,
we get

\mathbbm{1}_{\{|RY(j)|\leq M_{h-1}\}}=1\text{ a.s.},\forall j\in[d].

(27)

Proceeding with these observations, we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\|Q_{{\tt at},R}(Y)\|_{2}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{\left\lceil\frac{d}{s}\right\rceil}\sum_{j=(i-1)s+1}^{\min\{is,d\}}\mathbb{E}\left[(Q_{{\tt at},I}(RY)(j))^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{\{|RY(j)|\leq M_{h-1}\}}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{\left\lceil\frac{d}{s}\right\rceil}\sum_{j=(i-1)s+1}^{\min\{is,d\}}\mathbb{E}\left[(Q_{{\tt at},I}(RY)(j)-RY(j)+RY(j))^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{\{|RY(j)|\leq M_{h-1}\}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{\left\lceil\frac{d}{s}\right\rceil}\sum_{j=(i-1)s+1}^{\min\{is,d\}}\mathbb{E}\left[\left((Q_{{\tt at},I}(RY)(j)-RY(j))^{2}+RY(j)^{2}\right)\mathbbm{1}_{\{|RY(j)|\leq M_{h-1}\}}\right],

where the previous inequality uses the fact that, under the event
\{|RY(j)|\leq M_{h-1}\}
,

Q_{{\tt at},I}(RY)(j)
is an unbiased estimate of
RY(j)
. Namely,

\mathbb{E}\left[Q_{{\tt at},I}(RY)(j)\mathbbm{1}_{\{|RY(j)|\leq M_{h-1}\}}\mid R,Y\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[RY(j)\mathbbm{1}_{\{|RY(j)|\leq M_{h-1}\}}\mid R,Y\right].

Therefore, noting that
R
is a unitary matrix, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\|Q_{{\tt at},R}(Y)\|_{2}^{2}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{j\in[d]}(RY(i)-Q_{{\tt at},I}(RY)(j))^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{\{|RY(j)|\leq M_{h-1}\}}\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[\|Y\|_{2}^{2}\right].

To bound the first term on the right-side we have the following lemma.

Lemma 6.9
.

For an
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-valued random variable
Y
such that
\|Y\|_{2}^{2}\leq B^{2}
a.s.. Then, for
m
and
m_{0}
set to be
3B^{2}/d
and
(2B^{2}/d)\ln s
, respectively, we have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{j\in[d]}(RY(i)-Q_{{\tt at},I}(RY)(j))^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{\{|RY(j)|\leq M_{h-1}\}}\right]\leq B^{2}\cdot\frac{9+3\ln s}{(k-1)^{2}}.

Proof.

By Lemma
6.8
we have that all coordinates

RY(j)
are centered subgaussian random variable with variance
factor
B^{2}/d
. Thus, the parameters
m
and
m_{0}
of RATQ set as
in (
8
), and equal
3v
and
2v\ln s
, respectively,
where
v
is the variance factor of each subgaussian coordinate. The result follows by invoking Lemma
6.7
.
∎

Therefore, for any
Y
such that
\|Y\|_{2}^{2}\leq B^{2}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\|Q_{{\tt at},R}(Y)\|_{2}^{2}\right]\leq B^{2}\cdot\frac{9+3\ln s}{(k-1)^{2}}+B^{2},

whereby

\alpha_{2}(Q_{{\tt at},R})\leq B\sqrt{\frac{9+3\ln s}{(k-1)^{2}}+1}.

The worst-case bias of RATQ.

By (
27
) we have that the input always remains
in the dynamic-range of the quantizer, resulting in unbiased quantized
values. In other words,
\beta_{2}(Q_{{\tt at,R}})=0
.

4
Proof of Lemma
5.5

We first note AGUQ is used to quantize a scalar
Y
.
It follows from the description of the quantizer that

\mathbbm{1}_{\{|{Y}|\leq M_{g,h_{g}-1}\}}\mathbb{E}\left[Q_{\tt a}(Y)|Y\right]=\mathbbm{1}_{\{|{Y}|\leq M_{g,h_{g}-1}\}}Y,

(28)

and that
12
12

12

Once again, this follows from our convention that the outflow symbol is evaluated to
0
.

\mathbbm{1}_{\{|{Y}|>M_{g,h_{g}-1}\}}Q_{\tt a}(Y)=0.

(29)

The worst-case second moment of AGUQ.

Towards evaluating
\alpha(Q_{{\tt a}})
for AGUQ, for any
Y\in\mathbb{R}
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[Q_{\tt a}(Y)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[Q_{\tt a}(Y)^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{\{|Y|\leq M_{g,h_{g}-1}\}}\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[Q_{\tt a}(Y)^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{\{|Y|>M_{g,h-1}\}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[(Q_{\tt a}(Y)-Y+Y)^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{\{|Y|\leq M_{g,h_{g}-1}\}}\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[Q_{\tt a}(Y)^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{\{|Y|>M_{g,h_{g}-1}\}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[(Q_{\tt a}(Y)-Y)^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{\{|Y|\leq M_{g,h_{g}-1}\}}\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[Y^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{\{|Y|\leq M_{g,h_{g}-1}\}}\right],

where the last identity uses
(
29
), and the fact that
\mathbb{E}\left[(Q_{\tt a}(Y)-Y)Y\mathbbm{1}_{\{|Y|\leq M_{g,h-1}\}}|Y\right]=0,
which
follows from (
28
). From Lemma
6.4
it follows
that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[(Q_{{\tt a}}(Y)-Y)^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{\{|Y|\leq M_{g,h-1}\}}\right]\leq\frac{1}{4(k_{g}-1)^{2}}\left(B^{2}+\sum_{j=1}^{h-1}M_{j}^{2}P\left(|Y|>M_{g,j-1}\right)\right).

By Markov’s
inequality we get that for any random variable
Y
with
\mathbb{E}\left[Y^{2}\right]\leq B^{2}
, we have

P(|Y|>M_{g,j-1})\leq B^{2}/M^{2}_{g,j-1},
which further leads to

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[(Q_{{\tt a}}(Y)-Y)_{2}^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{\{|Y|\leq M_{g,h-1}\}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{B^{2}}{4(k_{g}-1)^{2}}+\sum_{j=1}^{h_{g}-1}\frac{M_{g,j}^{2}}{4(k_{g}-1)^{2}}\frac{B^{2}}{M_{g,j-1}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{B^{2}}{4(k_{g}-1)^{2}}+\frac{a_{g}(h_{g}-1)B^{2}}{4(k_{g}-1)^{2}}.

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[Q_{\tt a}(Y)^{2}\right]\leq\frac{B^{2}}{4(k_{g}-1)^{2}}+\frac{a_{g}(h_{g}-1)B^{2}}{4(k_{g}-1)^{2}}+\mathbb{E}\left[Y^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{\{|Y|\leq M_{g,h-1}\}}\right].

The result follows upon taking the supremum of the left-side over all
random variables
Y
with
\mathbb{E}\left[Y^{2}\right]\leq B^{2}
.

The worst-case bias of AGUQ.

Towards evaluating
\beta(Q_{\tt a})
, we note first using Jensen’s inequality that

\displaystyle\big|\mathbb{E}\left[Q_{\tt a}(Y)-Y\right]\big|

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\big|\mathbb{E}\left[Q_{\tt a}(Y)-Y|Y\right]\big|\right].

Then, for
Y
with
\mathbb{E}\left[Y^{2}\right]\leq B^{2}
, using (
28
) and Markov’s inequality, we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[|\mathbb{E}\left[Q_{\tt a}(Y)-Y|Y\right]|\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[|Y|\mathbbm{1}_{\{|Y|\geq M_{g,h-1}\}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\left[Y^{2}\right]P(|Y|\geq M_{g,h-1})}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{B^{2}}{M_{g,h-1}}.

(30)

Therefore, for any
Y
with
\mathbb{E}\left[Y^{2}\right]\leq B^{2}
, we have

\displaystyle\big|\mathbb{E}\left[Q_{\tt a}(Y)-Y\right]\big|\leq\sup_{Y\geq 0\text{a.s.}:\mathbb{E}\left[Y^{2}\right]\leq B^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\big|\mathbb{E}\left[Q_{\tt a}(Y)-Y|Y\right]\big|\right]\leq\frac{B^{2}}{M_{g,h-1}}.

The result follows upon taking the supremum of left-side over all
random variables
Y
with
\mathbb{E}\left[Y^{2}\right]\leq B^{2}
. ∎

5
Proof of Lemma
5.10

O(1)
expected precision.

Recall that the variable-length bit string is a concatenation of two bit strings: The first bit string represents the dynamic range
M_{g,j}
,
j\in\{0,\ldots h-1\}
; the second-bit string represents the uniform level within that dynamic range.

The first string uses the first
h
symbols of the Huffman codes corresponding to the geometric distribution with parameter
1/2.
Its code length can be bounded as follows.
By Markov’s
inequality, we have

P(|Y|>M_{g,j-1})\leq B^{2}/M^{2}_{g,j-1}=a_{g}^{-j}=2^{-j+1}.

For a symbol
j\in\{0,\cdots,h-1\}
representing the chosen dynamic range, let
\ell(j)
denote the length of the codeword of that symbol.
Therefore, the expected codelength
\mathbb{E}\left[L\right]
can be upper bounded as follows.

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[L\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=0}^{h-1}P(|Y|>M_{g,j-1})\ell(j)

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum_{j=0}^{h-1}2^{-j}\ell(j).

Since we will assign code-lengths
\ell(j)
as that assigned to the first
h
symbols of the Huffman code corresponding to the geometric distribtution with parameter
1/2
, we have
the following.

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[L\right]

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum_{j=0}^{h-1}2^{-j}\ell(j)

\displaystyle=4\sum_{j=0}^{h-1}2^{-j-1}\ell(j)

\displaystyle\leq 4(H(Z)+1),

where
Z
is the geometric distribution with parameter
1/2
. Above, the final inequality can be seen by the fact that expected code-length for Huffman codes for a particular pmf is upper bounded by one plus entropy of that pmf. This bounds the code-length of the first bit string by a constant.

Coming to the bounding the code-length of the second bit string, the expected code length of the second string is upper bounded by

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{h-1}P(|Y|>M_{g,j-1})\log(k_{g,j}+1)\leq 2\sum_{j=0}^{h-1}2^{-j}(j+1)\leq 12.

Worst-case second moment of AGUQ
+
.

The only change from the worst-case second moment upper bound calculations in the proof of Lemma
5.5
is that the number of uniform levels for different dynamic ranges is different. The rest of proof remains precisely the same, and is skipped.

Bias of AGUQ
+
.

The proof is identical to one in Lemma
5.5
, and is skipped.
∎

6
Proof of Theorems
5.3
and
5.4

Before we proceed with our lower bounds, we will set up some
notation. We consider quantizers of the form

Q(Y)=Q_{g}(\|Y\|_{2})Q_{s}(Y/\|Y\|_{2}).

Let
W(\cdot|y)
,
W_{g}(\cdot|y)
, and
W_{s}(\cdot|y)
, respectively,
denote the distribution of the output of quantizers
Q(y)
,
Q_{g}(y),
and
Q_{s}(y)
.
We prove a general lower bound for a quantizer satisfying Structural Assumptions
1-3 in Section
1
in terms of the precision
r
; Theorems
5.3
and
5.4

are obtained as corollaries of this
general lower bound.

Theorem 6.10
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{X}
contains the set
\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\|x\|_{2}\leq D/2\}
.
Consider a gain-shape quantizer
Q
of precision
r
satisfying the Assumptions 1-3 in Section
1
. Then, there exists an oracle
(f,O)\in\mathcal{O}
such that for any optimization protocol
\pi
using
T

iterations, we have

\mathcal{E}(f,O,\pi,Q)\geq\frac{DB}{2\sqrt{2}}\min\bigg\{\frac{1}{2^{r}},\frac{1}{4\cdot 2^{r/3}T^{1/3}},\frac{1}{2(2T)^{1/3}}\bigg\}.

Proof.

Consider the function
f_{\alpha}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
,
\alpha\in\{-1,1\}
given by

f_{\alpha}(x):=\delta\frac{B}{\sqrt{2}}|x(1)-\alpha D/2|,\quad\alpha\in\{-1,1\}.

Note that the functions
f_{1}
and
f_{-1}
are convex and depend only on the first
coordinate of
x
. Further, for
x\in\mathcal{X}
, the subgradient of
f_{\alpha}

is
-\delta\alpha Be_{1}/\sqrt{2}
, since
sign(x(1)-\alpha D/2)=-sign(\alpha)
, where
e_{1}
is the vector
[1,0,0,\dots,0]^{T}
. We consider oracles
O_{\alpha}
,
\alpha\in\{-1,1\}
, that produce noisy subgradient updates with distribution

\displaystyle P_{\alpha}\left(\frac{B}{\sqrt{2}}e_{1}\right)=\frac{1-\delta^{2}}{2},\quad P_{\alpha}\left(\frac{-B}{\sqrt{2}}e_{1}\right)=\frac{1-\delta^{2}}{2},\quad P_{\alpha}\big(-\frac{\alpha B}{\sqrt{2}\delta}e_{1}\big)=\delta^{2}.

It is easy to check that the oracle outputs satisfy (
5
)
and (
3
) described in Section
1
. That is, the output of
O_{\alpha}
is an unbiased estimate of the subgradient of
f_{\alpha}
, and the expected Euclidean norm square of the oracle output is bounded by
B^{2}
.

We now take recourse to the standard reduction of optimization to
hypothesis testing: To estimate the optimal value of
f_{1}
and

f_{-1}
to an accuracy
\delta
, the optimization protocol must
determine if the oracle outputs are generated by
P_{1}
or

P_{-1}
. However in order to distinguish between
P_{1}
or

P_{-1}
, the optimization protocol only has access to the quantized oracle outputs. Specifically, the protocol sees the samples from
Q(Y)
at every time step, where
Y
has distribution either
P_{1}
or
P_{-1}
.

Denoting by
P_{\alpha}W
the distribution
of the output
Q(Y)
when the input
Y
is
generated from
P_{\alpha}
,
we have from the standard reduction (see, for instance,
[
23
, Theorem 5.2.4]
) that

\max_{\alpha\in\{-1,1\}}\mathcal{E}(f,O,\pi,Q)\geq\frac{DB}{2\sqrt{2}}\delta\bigg(1-\sqrt{\frac{T}{2}\chi^{2}(P_{1}W,P_{-1}W}\bigg),

where
\displaystyle{\chi^{2}(P,Q)=\sum_{x}(P(x)-Q(x))^{2}/Q(x)}
denotes the chi-squared divergence between
P
and
Q
.

Note that Assumption 2 on the structure of the quantizer implies that when
M<B/{\delta\sqrt{2}}
, the distributions
P_{1}W
and
P_{-1}W
are the same. It follows that for every
\delta<\min\{\sqrt{B^{2}/2M^{2}},1\}
, the
left-side of the previous inequality exceeds
(DB/2\sqrt{2})\delta
, whereby

\displaystyle\max_{\alpha\in\{-1,1\}}\mathcal{E}(f,O,\pi,Q)\geq\frac{DB}{2\sqrt{2}}\min\bigg\{\frac{B}{\sqrt{2}M},1\bigg\}.

(31)

Next, we consider the following modification of the previous
construction in the case when
{B}/\sqrt{2}<m
:

\displaystyle P_{\alpha}\left(\frac{B}{\sqrt{2}}e_{1}\right)=\frac{1-\delta^{1+y}}{2},\quad P_{\alpha}\left(\frac{-B}{\sqrt{2}}e_{1}\right)=\frac{1-\delta^{1+y}}{2},\quad P_{\alpha}\big(-\frac{\alpha B}{\sqrt{2}\delta^{y}}e_{1}\big)=\delta^{1+y}.

for
y\in[0,1]
. Once again, the oracle outputs satisfy (
5
)
and (
3
) described in Section
1
. In this case, the vector
Y\sim P_{\alpha}
has entries with
\ell_{2}
norm at the most
B/(\sqrt{2}\delta^{y})
. We set
y
such that this value is less than
m
and
\chi^{2}(P_{1}W,P_{-1}W)
is minimized. Note that if
B/(\delta^{y}\sqrt{2d})<m
, then
{\tt supp}(Q_{g}(\|a\|))\subseteq\{0,m\}
for all the
a
’s in the support of
P_{1}
or
P_{-1}
.

For all
z\neq 0
,
z\in{\tt supp}(Q(a))
, when
a
is in the support of
P_{1}
or
P_{-1}
, we have

\displaystyle W\big(z\mid a\big)=W_{g}\big(m\mid\|a\|_{2}\big)W_{s}\big(\frac{z}{m}\mid\frac{a}{\|a\|_{2}}\big).

Therefore,

\displaystyle P_{1}W(z)-P_{-1}W(z)

\displaystyle=\delta^{1+y}W_{g}\big(m\mid\frac{B}{\sqrt{2}\delta^{y}}\big)\left(W_{s}\big(\frac{z}{m}\mid-e_{1}\big)-W_{s}\big(\frac{z}{m}\mid e_{1}\big)\right)

\displaystyle P_{-1}W(z)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1-\delta^{1+y}}{2}W_{g}\big(m\mid\frac{B}{\sqrt{2}}\big)W_{s}\big(\frac{z}{m}\mid e_{1}\big)+\frac{1-\delta^{1+y}}{2}W_{g}\big(m\mid\frac{B}{\sqrt{2}}\big)W_{s}\big(\frac{z}{m}\mid-e_{1}\big).

Using the preceding two inequalities

\displaystyle\frac{(P_{1}W(z)-P_{-1}W(z))^{2}}{P_{-1}W(z)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\delta^{2+2y}W_{g}\big(m\mid\frac{B}{\sqrt{2}\delta^{y}}\big)^{2}\left(W_{s}\big(\frac{z}{m}\mid e_{1}\big)-W_{s}\big(\frac{z}{m}\mid-e_{1}\big)\right)^{2}}{\frac{1-\delta^{1+y}}{2}W_{g}\big(m\mid\frac{B}{\sqrt{2}}\big)W_{s}\big(\frac{z}{m}\mid e_{1}\big)+\frac{1-\delta^{1+y}}{2}W_{g}\big(m\mid\frac{B}{\sqrt{2}}\big)W_{s}\big(\frac{z}{m}\mid-e_{1}\big)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2\delta^{2+2y}}{1-\delta^{1+y}}\cdot\frac{W_{g}\big(m\mid\frac{B}{\sqrt{2}\delta^{y}}\big)^{2}\left(W_{s}\big(\frac{z}{m}\mid e_{1}\big)+W_{s}\big(\frac{z}{m}\mid-e_{1}\big)\right)}{W_{g}\big(m\mid\frac{B}{\sqrt{2}}\big)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2\delta^{2+y}}{1-\delta^{1+y}}\cdot W_{g}\big(m\mid\frac{B}{\sqrt{2}\delta^{y}}\big)\left(W_{s}\big(\frac{z}{m}\mid e_{1}\big)+W_{s}\big(\frac{z}{m}\mid-e_{1}\big)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2\delta^{2+y}}{1-\delta^{1+y}}\cdot\left(W_{s}\big(\frac{z}{m}\mid e_{1}\big)+W_{s}\big(\frac{z}{m}\mid-e_{1}\big)\right),

where the third inequality uses Assumption 3b for the quantizer in Section
1
, i.e., it uses

\frac{W_{g}\big(m\mid\frac{B}{\sqrt{2}\delta^{y}}\big)}{W_{g}\big(m\mid\frac{B}{\sqrt{2}}\big)}\leq\delta^{-y}.

For
z=0
,
z\in{\tt supp}(Q(a))
, when
a
is in the support of
P_{1}
or
P_{-1}
, we have

\displaystyle W\big(0\mid a\big)=W_{g}\big(0\mid\|a\|_{2}\big)+W_{g}\big(m\mid\|a\|_{2}\big)W_{s}\big(0\mid a/\|a\|_{2}\big).

Therefore, by similar calculations for
z\neq 0
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{(P_{1}W(0)-P_{-1}W(0))^{2}}{P_{-1}W(0)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\delta^{2+2y}W_{g}\big(m\mid\frac{B}{\sqrt{2}\delta^{y}}\big)^{2}\left(W_{s}\big(0\mid e_{1}\big)+W_{s}\big(0\mid-e_{1}\big)\right)^{2}}{(\frac{1-\delta^{1+y}}{2})W_{g}\big(m\mid\frac{B}{\sqrt{2}}\big)W_{s}\big(0\mid e_{1}\big)+(\frac{1-\delta^{1+y}}{2})W_{g}\big(m\mid\frac{B}{\sqrt{2}}\big)W_{s}\big(0\mid-e_{1}\big)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2\delta^{2+y}}{1-\delta^{1+y}}\left(W_{s}\big(0\mid e_{1}\big)+W_{s}\big(0\mid-e_{1}\big)\right).

In conclusion,

\displaystyle\chi^{2}(P_{1}W,P_{-1}W)\leq\frac{4\delta^{2+y}}{1-\delta^{1+y}}.

Now, if
\delta<1/2,
we have

\displaystyle\chi^{2}(P_{1}W,P_{-1}W)\leq 8\delta^{2+y}.

Upon setting
\delta=(16T)^{-1/(2+y)}
, which satisfies
\delta<1/2
for all
T
,
we get

\displaystyle\max_{\alpha\in\{-1,1\}}\mathcal{E}(f,O,\pi,Q)\geq\frac{DB}{2\sqrt{2}}\delta(1-\sqrt{4T\delta^{2+y}})=\frac{DB}{4\sqrt{2}}\left(\frac{1}{16T}\right)^{\frac{1}{2+y}}.

(32)

But we can only set
\delta
to this value if

\displaystyle\frac{B}{\sqrt{2}}\cdot\left({16T}\right)^{\frac{y}{2+y}}<m.

(33)

Thus, for each
y
such that (
33
) holds, we get (
32
).
Taking the the supremum of RHS in (
32
) over all
y\in[0,1]
such that (
33
) holds, we obtain whenever
B/\sqrt{2}\leq m,

\displaystyle\max_{\alpha\in\{-1,1\}}\mathcal{E}(f,O,\pi,Q)\geq\frac{DB}{2\sqrt{2}}\cdot\min\left\{\frac{1}{8}\sqrt{\frac{m\sqrt{2}}{BT}},\frac{1}{2(2T)^{1/3}}\right\},

where we use the following lemma proved in Section
3
.

Lemma 6.11
.

For
a,c>0,
and
b>1
.

\sup_{y\in[0,1]:a(b)^{y/(2+y)}<c.}a\left(\frac{1}{b}\right)^{\frac{1}{2+y}}=\min\left\{\sqrt{\frac{ca}{b}},\frac{a}{b^{\frac{1}{3}}}\right\}

Upon combining this bound with (
31
), we obtain

\sup_{(f,O)\in\mathcal{O}}\varepsilon(f_{\alpha},\pi^{QO})\geq\frac{DB}{2\sqrt{2}}\max\left\{\min\left\{\frac{cm}{M},1\right\},\min\left\{\frac{1}{8}\sqrt{\frac{1}{cT}},\frac{1}{2(2T)^{1/3}}\right\}\mathbbm{1}_{\{c<1\}}\right\},

where
c=B/(m\sqrt{2})
. By making cases
1\leq c
,
\frac{1}{8(2T)^{1/3}}\leq c<1
, and
c<\frac{1}{8(2T)^{1/3}}
, and using the fact that for
a,b\geq 0
,
\max\{a,b\}\geq a^{1/3}b^{2/3}
in the second case, we get

\sup_{(f,O)\in\mathcal{O}}\varepsilon(f_{\alpha},\pi^{QO})\geq\frac{DB}{2\sqrt{2}}\min\bigg\{1,\frac{1}{(M/m)},\frac{1}{4(M/m)^{1/3}T^{1/3}},\frac{1}{2(2T)^{1/3}}\bigg\}.

By Assumption 3 in Section
1
, we know that
\frac{M}{m}\leq 2^{r}
. Therefore,

\sup_{(f,O)\in\mathcal{O}}\varepsilon(f_{\alpha},\pi^{QO})\geq\frac{DB}{2\sqrt{2}}\min\bigg\{\frac{1}{2^{r}},\frac{1}{4(2)^{r/3}T^{1/3}},\frac{1}{2(2T)^{1/3}}\bigg\}.

∎

Theorem
5.4
follows as an immediate corollary;
Theorem
5.3
, too, is obtained by noting that

\sup_{(f,O)\in\mathcal{O}}\varepsilon(f_{\alpha},\pi^{QO})<\frac{3DB}{\sqrt{T}}

holds only if
\sqrt{T}<2^{r}
.

7
Remaining proofs for the main results

1
Analysis of CUQ: Proof of Lemmas
6.1
and
6.2

Proof of Lemma
6.1
:

Denoting by
\mathscr{B}_{j,\ell}
the event
\big\{{Y}(j)\in[B_{M,k}(\ell),B_{M,k}(\ell+1))\big\}
, we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{j\in[d]}\big(Q_{{\tt u}}(Y)(j)-{Y(j)}\big)^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{\{{|Y(j)|}\leq M\}}\mid Y\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in[d]}\sum_{\ell=0}^{k-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\big(Q_{{\tt u}}(Y)(j)-{Y}(j)\big)^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathscr{B}_{j,\ell}}\mid{Y}\right]\mathbbm{1}_{\{{|Y(j)|}\leq M\}}.

For the term inside the summation on
the right-side, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\big(Q_{{\tt u}}(Y)(j)-{Y}(j)\big)^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathscr{B}_{j,\ell}}\mid{Y}\right]

\displaystyle=\left((B_{M,k}(\ell+1)-{Y}(j))^{2}\frac{{Y}(j)-B_{M,k}(\ell)}{B_{M,k}(\ell+1)-B_{M,k}(\ell)}\right)\mathbbm{1}_{\mathscr{B}_{j,\ell}}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+\left((B_{M,k}(\ell)-{Y}(j))^{2}\frac{B_{M,k}(\ell+1)-{Y}(j)}{B_{M,k}(\ell+1)-B_{M,k}(\ell)}\right)\mathbbm{1}_{\mathscr{B}_{j,\ell}}

\displaystyle=\left(B_{M,k}(\ell+1)-{Y}(j))({Y}(j)-B_{M,k}(\ell)\right)\mathbbm{1}_{\mathscr{B}_{j,\ell}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{4}\left(B_{M,k}(\ell+1)-B_{M,k}(\ell)\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{M^{2}}{(k-1)^{2}},

(34)

where the inequality uses the GM-AM inequality and the final identity
is simply by the definition of
B_{M,k}(\ell)
. Upon combining the
bounds above, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{j\in[d]}\big(Q_{{\tt u}}(Y)(j)-{Y(j)}\big)^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{\{{|Y(j)|}\leq M\}}\mid Y\right]\leq\frac{dM^{2}}{(k-1)^{2}}\cdot\frac{1}{d}\sum_{j\in[d]}\mathbbm{1}_{\{{|Y(j)|}\leq M\}}.

∎

Proof of Lemma
6.2

The proof is the same as the proof of Lemma
6.1
, except that
we need to set
d=1
and replace the identity used
in(
1
) with

B_{M,k}(\ell+1)-B_{M,k}(\ell)=\frac{M}{k-1}.

2
Proof of Lemma
6.8

For the rotation matrix
R=(1/\sqrt{d})HD
, each entry of
RY(j)
of
the rotated matrix has the same distribution as
(1/\sqrt{d})V^{T}Y
,
where
V=[V(1),...,V(d)]^{T}
has independent Rademacher entries. We
will use this observation to bound the moment generating function of

RY(i)
conditioned on
Y
. Towards that end, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\lambda RY(i)}\mid Y\right]

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{d}\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\lambda V(i)Y(i)/\sqrt{d}}\mid Y\right]

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{d}\frac{e^{\lambda Y(i)/\sqrt{d}}+e^{-\lambda Y(i)/\sqrt{d}}}{2}

\displaystyle\leq\prod_{i=1}^{d}e^{\lambda^{2}Y(i)^{2}/2d}

\displaystyle=e^{\lambda^{2}\|Y\|_{2}^{2}/2d},

where the first identity follows
from independence of
V(i)
s and the first inequality follows by the
fact that
(e^{x}+e^{-x})/2
is less than
e^{x^{2}/2}
, which in turn
can be seen from the Taylor series expansion of these terms. Thus, we
have proved the following:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\lambda RY(i)}\mid Y\right]\leq e^{\lambda^{2}\|Y\|_{2}^{2}/2d},\quad\forall\lambda\in\mathbb{R},\forall i\in[d].

(35)

Note that
\|Y\|_{2}^{2}
can be further bounded by
B^{2}
, which along
with (
35
) leads to

\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\lambda RY(i)}\right]\leq e^{\lambda^{2}B^{2}/2d}\quad\forall\lambda\in\mathbb{R},\forall i\in[d].

Using this inequality and the observation that
\mathbb{E}\left[RY(i)\right]=0
,
we note that
RY(i)
is a centered subgaussian with a variance
parameter
B^{2}/d
. The second statement of the lemma trivially
follows from Lemma
6.6
.
∎.

3
Proof of Lemma
6.11

For any
y\in[0,1]
such that
ab^{y/(2+y)}<c
, we have

ab^{y/(2+y)}<\min\left\{c,ab^{1/3}\right\}
. By multiplying by

a/b
on both sides and taking square root, we get

\frac{a}{b^{\frac{1}{2+y}}}<\min\left\{\sqrt{\frac{ca}{b}},\frac{a}{b^{1/3}}\right\},

which gives

\sup_{y\in[0,1]:a(b)^{y/(2+y)}<c.}\frac{a}{b^{\frac{1}{2+y}}}\leq\min\left\{\sqrt{\frac{ca}{b}},\frac{a}{b^{1/3}}\right\}.

Making cases
ab^{1/3}\geq c
and
ab^{1/3}<c
, we note that the
supremum on the left-side equals the right-side in both the cases.
∎

8
Concluding Remarks

In this chapter, we developed quantizers for communication-constrained optimization over Euclidean spaces. The problem here essentially reduces to minimizing the worst-case
\ell_{2}
norm,
\alpha_{2}(Q)
or
\alpha_{2}^{\tt m}(Q)
, of the quantized gradient under the constraint that worst-case
\ell_{2}
bias between the quantized gradient and input gradient,
\beta_{2}(Q)
or
\beta_{2}^{\tt m}(Q)
, is small and the output of the quantizer can be represented in
r
bits. In fact, in the next chapter, we will see that for designing gradient quantizers for communication-constrained optimization over
\ell_{p}
spaces, we need to solve a similar problem where the
\ell_{q}
norms are considered instead, where
q
is the Hölder conjugate of
p
.
Since the only knowledge we have of the input gradient to be quantized is either an almost sure or mean square-bound on the
\ell_{2}
norm of the gradient, we first preprocess the gradient to have some handle over the distribution of the gradient coordinates. We do this by randomly rotating the gradient input since it divides the overall gradient value equally across coordinates. We believe that without any more information on gradient distribution, this is a reasonable preprocessing. We will resort to this idea of random rotation once again in Chapter
4
. Another idea we will pick up from the quantizer design in this chapter is that of adaptive quantization. As we saw in our achievability proofs, adaptive gradient quantization became crucial to come up with tight upper bounds. We will see in Chapter
5
that this idea can also be used for classic information theory problems such as the Gaussian rate-distortion problem.

Chapter 3
Communication-Constrained Optimization over
\ell_{p}
Spaces

1
Synopsis

In this chapter, we study communication-constrained optimization for
\ell_{p}
lipschitz and convex function family. For this class of functions, we characterize the minimum
precision to which the oracle output must be quantized to retain the unrestricted
convergence rates? We characterize this precision for every

p\geq 1
by accessing the information theoretic lower bounds derived in Chapter
1
and by
providing quantizers that (almost) achieve these lower bounds. Our
quantizers are new and easy to implement. In particular, our
results are exact for
p=2
and
p=\infty
, showing the minimum
precision needed in these settings are
\Theta(d)
and
\Theta(\log d)
, respectively. The latter result is surprising since recovering
the gradient vector will require
\Omega(d)
bits.

The results presented in this Chapter are from
[
67
]
.

2
Introduction

In this chapter, we develop new algorithms to match the lower-bounds for communication-constrained optimization over
\ell_{p}
spaces. Specifically, we study communication-constrained optimization for convex and
\ell_{p}
lipschitz function family. We study this problem in the
high-precision regime
introduced in Chapter
2
. That is, we ask what is the minimum precision to which the subgradient estimates’ supplied by the oracle must be quantized so that we can attain the convergence of the classic, unrestricted setting. We derive a lower bound on this precision using the lower bounds on optimization error derived in Chapter
1
. As our main contribution, we propose simple, efficient subgradient quantization algorithms which along with appropriate mirror decent algorithms match these lower bounds.

1
Main Contributions

We show that for
p\in[1,2]
and
p\geq 2
, respectively, roughly
d

and
d^{2/p}\log(d^{1-2/p}+1)
bits are necessary and sufficient
for retaining the standard convergence rates. These bounds are tight
upto an
O(\log d)
factor, in general, but are exact for
p=2
and

p=\infty
. Prior work has only considered the problem for the
Euclidean case, and not for general
\ell_{p}
geometry. Note that in the previous Chapter, we show that RATQ along with PSGD requires a precision of
O(d\log\log\log\ln^{*}d)
per iteration to attain the classic convergence rate. In this chapter we get rid of the nagging
\log\log\log\ln^{*}d
factor and establish tight
bounds.

We use different quantizers for
p\geq 2
and
p\in[1,2]
. In the

p\geq 2
range, we use a quantizer we call SimQ
+
. SimQ
+
, in
turn, uses multiple repetitions of another quantizer we call SimQ
which expresses a vector as a convex combination of corner points of
an
\ell_{1}
ball. It is SimQ that yields an
O(\log d)
bit
quantizer for optimization over
\ell_{\infty}
. Also, SimQ
+

yields the exact upper bound in the
\ell_{2}
case. In the
[1,2]

range, we divide the vector into two parts with small and large
coordinates. We use a uniform quantizer for the first part and
RATQ of Chapter
2
for the second part.

The main observation in our analysis for upper bound is that the role
of quantizer in optimization is not to express the gradient with small
error. It suffices to have an unbiased estimate with appropriately
bounded norms.

2
Prior Work

A detailed literature review on the quantizer used in communication-constrained optimization is presented in Chapter
2
. Most of the literature in this area looks at the Euclidean setting. In the general setting of Information-constrained optimization, convex and
\ell_{p}
Lipschitz family was considered in
[
29
]
, in a statistical query setup, and
[
24
]
, in a local differential privacy setup.

Finally, we remark that while our quantizers are related to the ones used in prior works, our main contribution is to show that our specific design choices yield optimal precision. For instance, the quantizers in
[
33
]
express the input as a
convex combination of a set of points, similar to SimQ.
One of the quantizers in
[
33
]
uses a similar set of points as that of SimQ with a different scaling. However, the quantizers in
[
33
]
are designed keeping in mind other objectives, and they fall short of attaining the optimal precision guarantees of SimQ and SimQ
+
. SimQ is also closely related Maurey’s empirical method (see
[
76
]
, or
[
90
]
for a recent reference), however, the use in gradient quantization is new.

Orgainization

In the next section, we describe the setup and the structure of the schemes we will be employing. In Section
4
, we describe the main result of the paper – a characterization of the minimum precision required to attain classic convergence rate for all
p
. In Section
5
and
1
, we describe our quantizers used to achieve our upper bounds. Finally, we close with comments on achieving tight upper bounds for the lower bounds in Theorems
4.4
and
4.4
for all
p
and for generalizing our results to mean square bounded oracles in Section
7
.

3
Setup and preliminaries

1
Setup

We consider optimization domains
\mathcal{X}_{p}
such that
\ell_{p}
diameter is less than
D
. That is,

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{p}\in\mathbb{X}_{p}(D):=\{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}:\sup_{x,y\in\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}\|x-y\|_{p}\leq D.\}

(1)

For the domain of optimization
\mathcal{X}_{p}
, we develop subgradient compression schemes for function and oracle families given by
\mathcal{O}_{{\tt c},p}
, which are defined in Definition
3.3
.

We want to study communication-constrained optimization for
\mathcal{O}_{{\tt c},p}
in the high precision regime.
Thus,
the fundamental quantity of interest in this work is the
minimum precision to achieve the optimization accuracy of the classic case, denoted by
r^{*}(T,p)
. Symbolically,

\displaystyle r^{*}(T,p)

\displaystyle:=\inf\{r\in\mathbb{N}:\sup_{\mathcal{X}\in\mathbb{X}_{p}(D)}\mathcal{E}^{*}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{O}_{{\tt c},p},T,\mathcal{W}_{{\tt com},r})\leq\mathcal{U}(T,p)\},

(2)

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}(T,p)

\displaystyle:=\frac{4c_{1}d^{1/2-1/p}DB}{\sqrt{T}},\quad\forall p\in(2,\infty],

(3)

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}(T,p)

\displaystyle:=\frac{4c_{1}\sqrt{\log d}DB}{\sqrt{T}},\quad\forall p\in[1,2),

and
\sup_{\mathcal{X}\in\mathbb{X}_{p}(D)}\mathcal{E}^{*}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{O}_{{\tt c},p},T,\mathcal{W}_{{\tt com},r})
is as defined in Chapter
1
.
Recall that
\mathcal{U}(T,p)
denotes the classic convergence rate for the family
\mathcal{O}_{{\tt c},p}
given in Theorem
3.5
, where the oracle output is available as it is to the optimization algorithm, without any quantization.

2
Quantizer performance for finite precision optimization

As described in Section
2
, we restrict to memoryless quantization schemes, where the same quantizer will be used for each new subgradient vector, without any information about the previous updates. Also recall from Section
2
, our nonadaptive channel selection strategy is simply denote by quantizer
Q
. Further, the optimization error for a function
f
and oracle
O
when employing a first order optimization
\pi
and quantizer
Q
is given by

\mathcal{E}(f,O,\pi,Q)=\mathbb{E}\left[f(x_{T})\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[f(x^{\ast})\right].

We now define
\alpha_{p}(Q)
, which generalizes the definition of
\alpha_{2}(Q)
in Chapter
2
, to charactize the performance of a quantizer
Q
for optimization of convex and
\ell_{p}
lipschitz funciton class. Since we restrict to unbiased quantizers, we don’t need to define
\beta
.

\displaystyle{\alpha_{p}(Q):=\sup_{Y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\|Y\|_{q}^{2}\leq B^{2}\text{ a.s.}}\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\left[\|Q(Y)\|_{2}^{2}\right]},\quad p\in(2,\infty]},

\displaystyle{\alpha_{p}(Q):=\sup_{Y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\|Y\|_{q}^{2}\leq B^{2}\text{ a.s.}}\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\left[\|Q(Y)\|_{q}^{2}\right]},\quad p\in[1,2].}

Note that for all
p\geq 1
, the composed oracle
QO

satisfies assumption (
5
). We employ the stochastic mirror descent (SMD) algorithm with mirror maps given by Remarks
1

and
2
to use the output from the composed oracle. The algorithm’s description is given in Algorithm

1
.
Recall that for a mirror map
\Phi
, the Bregman divergence associated with
\Phi
is defined as

D_{\Phi}(x,y)\colon=\Phi(x)-\Phi(y)-\langle\nabla\Phi(y),x-y\rangle.

1:

Require:

x_{0}\in\mathcal{X},\eta\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
,
T
and
access to composed oracle
QO

2:

for

t=0
to
T-1

do

x_{t+1}=\arg\min_{x\in\mathcal{X}}(\eta_{t}\langle x,Q(\hat{g}(x_{t}))\rangle)+D_{\Phi_{a}}(x,x_{t}))

3:

Output:

\frac{1}{T}\cdot{\sum_{t=1}^{T}x_{t}}

Algorithm 1
:
Quantized SMD with quantizer
Q

Moreover, in view of Remarks
1

and
2
, we have the following convergence
guarantees for first-order stochastic optimization using gradients
quantized by
Q
.

Theorem 3.1
.

Consider a quantizer
Q
for the gradients. Then the algorithm
1
with mirror maps as in Remarks
1
and
2
and an unbiased quantizer
Q

performs as follows.

1.

For
2\geq p\geq 1
,

\displaystyle{\frac{c_{1}\sqrt{\log d}D\alpha_{p}(Q)}{\sqrt{T}}\geq\sup_{(f,O)\in\mathcal{O}_{{\tt c},p}}\mathcal{E}(f,O,\pi,Q);}

2.

For
p>2
,

\displaystyle{\frac{c_{1}d^{1/2-1/p}D\alpha_{p}(Q)}{\sqrt{T}}\geq\sup_{(f,O)\in\mathcal{O}_{{\tt c},p}}\mathcal{E}(f,O,\pi,Q).}

Proof.

The proof straightaway follows from Theorem
3.5
and Remarks
1
and
2
. For completeness, we provide the details below.

First statement simply follows by noting that the bounds in Theorem
3.2
hold when instead of
\|\hat{g}(x)\|_{q}\leq B,
we have
\mathbb{E}\left[\|\hat{g}(x)\|_{q}^{2}\right]\leq B^{2}
and then using definition of
\alpha_{p}.
The second statement simply follows by noting that the bounds in Theorem
3.2
are obtained by employing PSGD. Thus it suffices to only have a bound on
\mathbb{E}\left[\|\hat{g}(x)\|_{2}^{2}\right]
and then using the definition of
\alpha_{p}.

∎

An interesting insight offered by the result above, which is perhaps simple in
hindsight, is that even when dealing with
\ell_{p}
oracles for
p>2
, we only need to be concerned about the expected
\ell_{2}
norm of
the quantizers output. This follows from the fact that PSGD is the optimal optimization algorithm for
p>2
and it’s convergence rate is only concerned with the
\ell_{2}
norm of the quantizers output. It is this insight that leads to the realization that SimQ
+
is optimal for these settings.

In the rest of the Chapter, we design unbiased, fixed length
quantizers which have
\alpha_{p}(\cdot)
of the same order as

B
. Then, using Theorem
3.1
the quantized updates give the
same convergence guarantees as that of the classical case, which
leads to upper bounds for
r^{*}(T,p)
. Further, we using Theorems
4.4
and
4.5
, we derive lower bounds for

r^{*}(T,p)
to prove optimality of our quantizers.

4
Main Result: Characterization of
r^{*}(T,p)

The main result of this Chapter is the almost complete
characterization of
r^{*}(T,p)
. We divide the result into cases

p\in[1,2]
and
p\geq 2
; as mentioned earlier, we use different
quantizers for these two cases.

Theorem 4.1
.

For stochastic optimization using
T
accesses to a first-order
oracle, the following bounds for
r^{*}(T,p)
hold.

1.

For
p>2
, we have

\displaystyle d^{2/p}\log

\displaystyle\,(2e\cdot d^{1-2/p}+2e)\geq r^{*}(T,p)\geq\left(\frac{c_{0}}{4c_{1}}\cdot d^{1/p}\right)^{2}\vee 2\log\left(\frac{c_{0}}{4c_{1}}\cdot d^{1/2}\right).

2.

For
2\geq p\geq 1,
we have

\displaystyle d\left(\left\lceil\log(2\sqrt{2}{\Delta_{1}}^{1/q}+2)\right\rceil+3\right)+\Delta_{2}\geq~r^{*}(T,p)\geq\left(\frac{c_{0}}{4c_{1}\sqrt{\log d}}\right)^{2}\cdot d,

where

\Delta_{1}=\left\lceil\log\left(2+\sqrt{18+6\ln\Delta_{2}}\cdot d^{1/2-1/q}\right)\right\rceil
and
\Delta_{2}=\left\lceil\log(1+\ln^{*}({d}/{3}))\right\rceil.

Note that for
p>2
the upper bounds and lower bounds for
r^{*}(T,p)
are off by nominal factor of
\log(d^{1-2/p}+1)
. Also, for
p\in[1,2]
the bounds are roughly off by
O(\log d\cdot\log(\log d^{1/2-1/q})^{1/q})
(ignoring the
\log^{*}d
terms).

We present the quantizers achieving these upper bounds, and the
proof of the upper bounds, in the next two sections. For
p>2
,
we use a quantizer SimQ and its extension SimQ
+
, presented in
Section
5
. For
p\in[1,2]
, we use a combination of
uniform quantization and the quantizer RATQ
from previous chapter, presented in
Section
6
.
The lower bounds on
r^{*}(T,p)
follow immediately by the lower bounds in Theorem
4.5
and
4.4
.

We highlight the most interesting features of the result above in
separate remarks below.

Remark 24

(
r^{*}(T,p)
is independent of
T
)
.

Theorem
4.1
shows that
r^{*}(T,p)
is a function only of
p

and
d
, and is
independent of
T
. The number of queries
T
is a proxy for the desired
optimization accuracy. Therefore, the fact that
r^{*}(T,p)
is
independent of such a parameter is interesting. We note, however,
that for oracle models with milder assumptions, such as mean square
bounded oracles, this may not hold. In fact, the results of previous chapter suggest that for mean square bounded oracles
r^{*}(T,2)
is dependent on
T
.

Remark 25

(Optimality for
p=\infty
)
.

Our bounds match for
p=\infty
, namely our quantizer SimQ offers optimal
convergence rate with gradient updates at the least precision. A
surprising observation is that this precision is merely
O(\log d)
,
much smaller than
O(d)
bits needed to recover the gradient vector
under any reasonable loss function.

Remark 26

(Optimality for
p=2
)
.

The high-precision regime for
p=2
was already considered in the previous chapter. Both
[
9
]
and

[
88
]
give variable-length quantization schemes
to exactly achieve the lower bound on
r^{*}(T,p)
, but the worst-case precision can be
order-wise greater than
d
. The quanitzer RATQ from the previous Chapter was within a small factor of

O(\log\log\log\ln^{*}d)
of this lower bound. In this chapter, we remove
this nagging factor using a different fixed-length quantizer SimQ
+
.

Remark 27

(Fixed precision)
.

The quantizer RATQ remains
optimal upto a factor of
O(\sqrt{\log\ln^{*}d})
for the more general problem of characterizing
\displaystyle{\sup_{\mathcal{X}\in\mathbb{X}_{p}(D)}\mathcal{E}^{*}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{O}_{{\tt c},p},T,\mathcal{W}_{{\tt com},r})}

for any precision
r
less than
d
bits. In this
setting of small precision, the performance of SimQ
+
is much worse.

5
Our quantizers for
p>2

We present our quantizer SimQ and its extension SimQ
+
. The former
is seen to be optimal for
p=\infty
while the latter for
p=2
.

1
An optimal quantizer for
p=\infty

1:

Input
Y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, Parameter
B

2:

i^{*}=\begin{cases}i\quad w.p.\quad|Y(i)|/B\\
0\quad w.p.\quad 1-\|Y\|_{1}/B\end{cases}

3:

if

i^{*}\in[d]

then

4:

j^{*}=sign(Y(i^{*}))

5:

else

6:

j^{*}=1

7:

Output:

Q^{{\tt e}}_{{\tt SimQ}}(Y;B)=i^{*}\cdot j^{*}

Figure 2
:
Encoder
Q^{{\tt e}}_{{\tt SimQ}}(Y;B)
for
SimQ

1:

Input
i^{\prime}\in\{-d,-(d-1),\cdots 0,\cdots,d\}

2:

if

i^{\prime}\neq 0

then

3:

Z=Bsign(i^{\prime})e_{|i^{\prime}|}

4:

else

5:

Z=0

6:

Output:

Q^{{\tt d}}_{{\tt SimQ}}(i^{\prime};B)=Z

Algorithm 3
:
Decoder
Q^{{\tt d}}_{{\tt SimQ}}(i^{\prime};B)
for
SimQ

Simplex Quantizer (
SimQ
)

Our first quantizer SimQ is described in Algorithms
2

and
3
. For
p=\infty
, our quantizer’s input vector
Y

is an unbiased estimate of the subgradient of the function at the
point queried and satisfies
\|Y\|_{1}\leq B
. SimQ takes such a

Y
as an input and produces an output vector which, too, satisfies
both these properties. The main idea behind SimQ is the fact that
any point inside the unit
\ell_{1}
ball can be represented as a convex
combination of at the most
2d
points:
\{e_{i},-e_{i}:i\in[d]\}
. With this observation, we can create an
unbiased estimate of the input vector using only these
2d

corner points along with the zero vector. Since all of these

2d+1
points have a
\ell_{2}
norm of at the most
B
, the output
vector, too, has a
\ell_{2}
norm of at the most
B
.

Theorem 5.1
.

Let
Q
be the quantizer SimQ described in Algorithms
2
,
3
. Then, for

Y
such that
\|Y\|_{1}\leq B~a.s.
,

Q(Y)
can be represented in
\log(2d+1)
bits,
\mathbb{E}\left[Q(Y)|Y\right]=Y
, and

\alpha_{\infty}(Q)\leq B
.

Proof.

Since
i^{*}\in[d]
and
j^{*}\in\{-1,1\}
, we can represent the output of
the encoder of SimQ using
\log(2d+1)
bits. Next, denoting
the quantizer SimQ by
Q
, note that

\mathbb{E}\left[Q(Y)|Y\right]=\sum_{i=1}^{d}B\cdot sign(Y(i))\cdot e_{i}\cdot\frac{|Y(i)|}{B}=Y,

namely SimQ is unbiased. To complete the proof, note that

\|Q(Y)\|_{2}^{2}\leq B^{2}~a.s.
.
∎

Theorem
5.1
along with Theorem
3.1
establishes
Theorem
4.1
for
p=\infty
.

2
Our Quantizer for
p\in[2,\infty)

For this case,
we need to quantize inputs that are bounded in

\ell_{q}
norm with
q\in(1,2]
so that the quantized output is unbiased and has small expected
\ell_{2}
norm square; we will use SimQ
+
to do this.

\textbf{SimQ}^{+}

The quantizer SimQ
+
outputs the average of
k
independent repetitions of the SimQ
quantizer for a given input vector. The input vectors
Y
satisfy

\|Y\|_{1}\leq Bd^{1/p}
. Therefore, we use SimQ with
parameter
Bd^{1/p}
instead of
B
. The repetitions help reduce the
error to compensate for the extra loss factor. Specifically, the output of
SimQ
+
denoted by
Q(Y)
is given by

\displaystyle Q(Y)=\frac{1}{k}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{k}Q^{i}_{\tt SimQ}(Y;Bd^{1/p}),

(4)

where
Q^{i}_{\tt SimQ}
are independent iterations of SimQ.

The next component of SimQ
+
is how the encoder of SimQ
+
expresses the output of these
k
copies of SimQ to attain compression. If represented naively, this
will require
O(d^{2/p}\log d)
. But we can do much better since we only need the average value of these entries. For that, we can simply
report the
type
of this vector – the frequency of each
index in the
k
length sequence. The signs of the
input coordinates for the non-zero entries can be sent separately.

Note that there are
d+1
indices
overall, as SimQ can pick any index from
\{0,\ldots d\}
.
Therefore, the total number of types is
{{d+k}\choose{k}}
, which
can at the most be
(\frac{ed+ek}{k})^{k}
bits. Hence, the precision
needed to represent the type is at the most
k\log e+k\log(\frac{d}{k}+1)
.

The type of the input can be used to determine a set
\mathcal{I}_{0}
of non-zero indices
that appear at least once. There are at most
k
such entries. Therefore, we can use
a binary vector of length
k
to store the signs for these entries. We use this representation in SimQ
+
, with the indices in
\mathcal{I}_{0}
represented in the vector in increasing order.

Theorem 5.2
.

For a
p\in[2,\infty)
, let
Q
be the quantizer SimQ
+
described in (
4
). Then, for

Y
such that
\|Y\|_{q}\leq B~a.s.,

Q(Y)
can be represented in
k\log e+k\log(\frac{d}{k}+1)+k
bits,
\mathbb{E}\left[Q(Y)|Y\right]=Y
, and

\displaystyle{\alpha_{p}(Q)\leq\sqrt{\frac{B^{2}d^{2/p}}{k}+B^{2}}}
.

Proof.

We already saw how to represent the output of the
k
copies of SimQ
using
k\log e+k\log(\frac{d}{k}+1)+k
bits.
For bounding
\alpha_{p}(Q)
, note from (
4
)
that SimQ
+
is an unbiased quantizer since SimQ is unbiased.
Further, denoting by
Q_{i}(Y)
the output
Q^{i}_{\tt SimQ}(Y;Bd^{1/p})
, we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\|Q(Y)\|_{2}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\|Q(Y)-Y\|_{2}^{2}\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[\|Y\|_{2}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[\|Q_{i}(Y)-Y\|_{2}^{2}|Y\right]\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[\|Y\|_{2}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\|Q_{1}(Y)-Y\|_{2}^{2}\right]}{k}+\mathbb{E}\left[\|Y\|_{2}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{d^{2/p}B^{2}}{k}+B^{2},

where the first identity uses the fact
that
Q(Y)
is an unbiased estimate of
Y
; the second uses the fact
that
Q_{i}(Y)-Y
are zero-mean, independent random variables when conditioned on
Y
;
the third uses the fact that
Q_{i}(Y)-Y
are identically distributed;
and the final inequality is by the performance of SimQ.
∎

The proof of upper bound for
p\in[2,\infty)
in Theorem
4.1
is completed by setting
k=d^{2/p}
and using Theorems
5.2
and
3.1
.

6
Our Quantizers for
p\in[1,2]

For p in
[1,2]
, the oracle yields unbiased subgradient estimates

Y
such that
\|Y\|_{q}\leq B
almost surely. Our goal is to
quantize such
Y
s in an unbiased manner and ensure that

\mathbb{E}\left[\|Q(Y)\|_{q}^{2}\right]
is
O(B^{2})
. It can be seen that a simple unbiased
uniform quantizer will achieve this using
d(\log d^{1/q}+1)
.
However, our goal here is to get a result that is stronger than this
baseline performance. To that end, we split the input vector
Y
in
two parts
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2}
with the first part having
\ell_{\infty}

norm less than
c
and the second part having less than
d/\Delta_{1}

nonzero coordinates. We use an “
\ell_{\infty}
ball quantizer” (a
uniform quantizer) for
Y_{1}
and an “
\ell_{2}
ball quantizer” for

Y_{2}
.

Specifically, set
\displaystyle{c:=\frac{B\Delta_{1}^{1/q}}{d^{1/q}}},
where
\Delta_{1}
is that in Theorem

4.1
. Then, define

\displaystyle Y_{1}:=\sum_{i=1}^{d}Y(i)\mathbbm{1}_{\{|Y(i)|\leq c\}}e_{i},\text{~~}Y_{2}:=\sum_{i=1}^{d}Y(i)\mathbbm{1}_{\{|Y(i)|>c\}}e_{i}.

(5)

Clearly,
\|Y_{1}\|_{\infty}\leq c
. Further, since
\|Y\|_{q}\leq B
, the number of nonzero coordinates in
Y_{2}
can be at the most

B^{q}/c^{q}=d/\Delta_{1}
. For quantizing
Y_{1}
, we use the coordinate-wise
uniform quantizer (CUQ) described in Section
1
. In order to quantize
Y_{1}
in (
5
),
we set the parameters of CUQ to

\displaystyle M=c,\quad\log(k+1)=\left\lceil\log(2\sqrt{2}\Delta_{1}^{1/q}+2)\right\rceil.

(6)

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
Q_{\tt u}
be the quantizer CUQ with parameters
M
and
k
set
as in (
6
). Then, for
Y
such that
\|Y\|_{q}\leq B~\text{a.s.}
and
Y_{1}
as that in (
5
),
Q_{\tt u}(Y_{1})
can
be represented in
d\left\lceil\log(2\sqrt{2}\Delta_{1}^{1/q}+2)\right\rceil
bits,

\mathbb{E}\left[Q_{\tt u}(Y_{1})\mid|Y\right]=Y_{1}
, and
\mathbb{E}\left[\|Q_{\tt u}(Y_{1})\|_{q}^{2}\right]\leq 3B^{2}
.

Proof.

CUQ requires a precision of
d\log(k+1)
, which coincides with the
statement above for our choice of
k
. To see unbiasedness, note
that CUQ is an unbiased quantizer as long as all the coordinates of
the input do not exceed
M
. Since we have set
M=c
and

\|Y_{1}\|_{\infty}=c
, this property holds. Finally, to show that

\mathbb{E}\left[\|Q_{\tt u}(Y_{1})\|_{q}^{2}\right]\leq 3B^{2}
, note that
\mathbb{E}\left[\|Q_{\tt u}(Y_{1})\|_{q}^{2}\right]\leq 2\mathbb{E}\left[\|Q_{\tt u}(Y_{1})-Y_{1}\|_{q}^{2}\right]+2\mathbb{E}\left[\|Y_{1}\|_{q}^{2}\right].
Also,

\mathbb{E}\left[\|Q_{\tt u}(Y_{1})-Y_{1}\|_{q}^{2}\right]\leq B^{2},

where we use the fact that for

M
set as in (
6
) we have that
|Q_{\tt u}(Y_{1})(i)-Y_{1}(i)|\leq\frac{2M}{(k-1)}
a.s.,
\forall i\in[d]
,
by the description of CUQ.
∎

In order to quantize
Y_{2}
, we indicate the coordinates with non-zero
entries. This takes less than
d
bits. Then, we quantize the
restriction
Y_{2}^{\prime}
of
Y_{2}
to these nonzero entries. Recall
that the dimension of
Y_{2}^{\prime}
is less than
d^{\prime}:={d}/{\Delta_{1}}
. Also, the
\ell_{2}
norm of
Y_{2}^{\prime}
is less
than
\|Y\|_{2}\leq\|Y\|_{q}d^{1/2-1/q}\leq Bd^{1/2-1/q}=:B^{\prime}
.

We need a quantizer
Q
such that
\mathbb{E}\left[\|Q(Y_{2}^{\prime})\|_{q}^{2}\right]
is

O(B^{2})
. As seen in the proof of Lemma
6.1
, one way to do this
is to ensure
\mathbb{E}\left[\|Q(Y_{2}^{\prime})-Y^{\prime}\|_{q}^{2}\right]
is
O(B^{2}),

which, in turn, can be ensured if

\mathbb{E}\left[\|Q(Y_{2}^{\prime})-Y_{2}^{\prime}\|_{2}^{2}\right]
is
O(B^{2})
. To achieve
this, we can use an unbiased quantizer for the unit
\ell_{2}
ball in

\mathbb{R}^{d}
, which can quantize it to an MSE of
O(1/d^{1-2/q})
using

O(d\log(d^{1/2-1/q})
bits.
We note that SimQ
+
, while optimal for the stochastic optimization
use-case, does not yield the required scaling of bits in MSE. A natural
candidate quantizer is RATQ, which is, in fact,
close to information theoretically optimal.
We
set the parameters of RATQ in terms of

B^{\prime}
and
d^{\prime}
.
We set

\displaystyle m=\frac{3{B^{\prime}}^{2}}{d^{\prime}},\quad m_{0}=\frac{2{B^{\prime}}^{2}}{d^{\prime}}\cdot\ln s,\quad\log h=\left\lceil\log(1+\ln^{\ast}(d^{\prime}/3))\right\rceil,

\displaystyle s=\log h,\quad\log(k+1)=\Delta_{1}.

(7)

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
Q_{{\tt at},R}
be the quantizer RATQ with parameters set as (
6
).
Then, for
Y
such that
\|Y\|_{q}\leq B~{a.s.}
and
Y_{2}^{\prime}
the restriction of
Y_{2}
in (
5
),
Q_{{\tt at},R}(Y_{2}^{\prime})
can be represented in
2d+\Delta_{2}
bits,
\mathbb{E}\left[Q_{{\tt at},R}(Y_{2}^{\prime})\mid|Y\right]=Y_{2}^{\prime}
, and
\mathbb{E}\left[\|Q_{{\tt at},R}(Y_{2}^{\prime})\|_{q}^{2}\right]\leq 3B^{2}.

Proof.

First, we note that the output of RATQ can be represented in
\left\lceil d^{\prime}/s\right\rceil(\log h)+d\log(k+1)
bits, which, in this case, is less than

\displaystyle\frac{d}{\Delta_{1}\log h}\cdot\left(\log h\right)+\log h+\left(\frac{d}{\Delta_{1}}\log(k+1)\right)\leq 2d+\Delta_{2}.

For unbiasedness, note that for our choice of
m,m_{0},h
, RATQ is always an unbiased quantizer of the input.
Finally, for showing
\mathbb{E}\left[\|Q_{{\tt at},R}(Y_{2}^{\prime})\|_{q}^{2}\right]\leq 3B^{2}
,
we note that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\|Q_{{\tt at},R}(Y_{2}^{\prime})\|_{q}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathbb{E}\left[\|Q_{{\tt at},R}(Y_{2}^{\prime})-Y_{2}^{\prime}\|_{q}^{2}\right]+2\mathbb{E}\left[\|Y_{2}^{\prime}\|_{q}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathbb{E}\left[\|Q_{{\tt at},R}(Y_{2}^{\prime})-Y_{2}^{\prime}\|_{q}^{2}\right]+2B^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathbb{E}\left[\|Q_{{\tt at},R}(Y_{2}^{\prime})-Y_{2}^{\prime}\|_{2}^{2}\right]+2B^{2}.

The proof will be complete upon showing that
\mathbb{E}\left[\|Q_{{\tt at},R}(Y_{2}^{\prime})-Y_{2}^{\prime}\|_{2}^{2}\right]\leq B^{2}/2
, towards
which we apply Lemma
6.9
to get

\mathbb{E}\left[\|Q_{{\tt at},R}(Y_{2}^{\prime})-Y_{2}^{\prime}\|_{2}^{2}\right]\leq B^{2}d^{1-2/q}\cdot\frac{9+3\ln s}{(k-1)^{2}},

and substituting our choice of
k
.
∎

The overall quantizer
Q
of input vector
Y
is the sum of quantized
outputs of
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2}
. By Lemmas
6.1
and
6.2
, the
quantized output of
Y
can be represented in
d\left(\left\lceil\log(2\sqrt{2}{\Delta_{1}}^{1/q}+2)\right\rceil+3\right)+\Delta_{2}
bits
1
1

1

This
accounts for the communication needed to send the nonzero indices of

Y_{2}
, too.
