Proofs

1
Proof of Theorem
3.2

We establish the expression for average age given in
(
12
) for the more general class of randomized
schemes; Theorem
3.2
will follow upon setting

\theta(x)=1
, for all
x\in\mathcal{X}
. Recall that the symbol
\emptyset
is available only in the extended model in Section
7
, and not in the original model discussed in rest of the paper.
Note that the formula for
average age given in Theorem
3.2
is similar in form to
the expressions for average age derived in other settings;
see
[
51
]
for an example.

We will first set up some notation.
Let
S_{0}:=0
and

\displaystyle S_{k}:=\inf\{t>S_{k-1}:U(t)>U(t-1)\},~k\in\mathbb{N}.

Namely,
S_{k}
is the time at which the decoder updates
its estimate for the symbol for the
k
th time.
Recall that
U(t)
is incremented only
on successful reception at the receiver and is strictly increasing in
t
.
For brevity, we introduce the
notation
Y_{k}:=S_{k}-S_{k-1}

for the time between the
(k-1)
th and the
k
th information update at the decoder.
Further, denote by
Z_{k}:=S_{k}-U(S_{k})
the age at time
S_{k}
, which is simply the time taken for
the successful reception of the symbol
9
9

9

This must be a symbol in
\mathcal{X}
and not
\emptyset
by the definition of
S_{k}
.

x\in\mathcal{X}
transmitted at time
U(S_{k})
.
Also, denote by
R_{k}
the sum of instantaneous age between
S_{k-1}
and
S_{k}
(the
k
th reward), namely

\displaystyle R_{k}:=\sum_{t=S_{k-1}+1}^{S_{k}}(t-U(t)).

Heuristically, our proof can be understood as follows. We note that the asymptotic average age is
roughly

\frac{\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}R_{k}}{\lim_{k\to\infty}S_{k}}.

It is easy to see that
\{Y_{k}\}_{k=1}^{\infty}
is an iid sequence. Thus, if

\{R_{k}\}_{k=1}^{\infty}
, too, was an iid sequence, we would obtain the
asymptotic average age to be
\mathbb{E}\left[R_{1}\right]/{\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{1}\right]}
by the standard
Renewal Reward Theorem
[
81
]
. Unfortunately, this
is not the case. But it turns out that the dependence in sequence

\{R_{k}\}
is only between consecutive terms. Therefore, we can obtain
the same conclusion as above by dividing the sum
\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}R_{k}
into the sum of odd terms and even terms, each of which is in
turn a sum of iid random variables.

We will now proceed to prove that dependence in
R_{k}
is between consecutive terms.
Since
U(t)
remains
U(S_{k-1})
for all
t<S_{k}
, we get for
k\geq 1
that

\displaystyle R_{k}

\displaystyle=\frac{(S_{k}-S_{k-1}-1)(S_{k}-S_{k-1})}{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+(S_{k}-S_{k-1}-1)\cdot(S_{k-1}-U(S_{k-1}))

\displaystyle\hskip 128.0374pt+S_{k}-U(S_{k})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}Y_{k}^{2}+Y_{k}\left(Z_{k-1}-\frac{1}{2}\right)+Z_{k}-Z_{k-1},

(19)

with
Z_{0}
set to
0
.

Note that since the source sequence
\{X_{n}\}
is iid and the randomization
\theta
is stationary, the sequences
Y_{k}
and
Z_{k}

are iid, too.
Therefore, the
(R_{2n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(R_{2n+1})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
are both
10
10

10

The initial term
R_{1}
has a different distribution since
Z_{0}=0
.

iid sequences with
\mathbb{E}\left[R_{2n}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[R_{2n+1}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[R_{2}\right]
for all
n
.

Using this observation, we can obtain the following expression for the average age:

\bar{A}(e,\theta)=\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[R_{2}\right]}{\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{1}\right]}.

(20)

Before we prove (
20
), which is the main ingredient of
our proof, we evaluate the expression on the right-side.

For
\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{1}\right]
, note that
Y_{1}
gets incremented by
\ell(\emptyset)
each
time
\emptyset
is sent, and gets incremented finally by
\ell(x)

once a symbol
x\in\mathcal{X}
is sent. Thus,
Y_{1}
takes the value

\ell(x)+r\ell(\emptyset)
with probability

(1-\mathbb{E}\left[\theta(X)\right])^{r}\theta(x)P(x)
.
Denoting
\mathbb{N}_{0}=\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
, we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{1}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\sum_{r\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}(\ell(x)+r\ell(\phi))P(x)\theta(x)(1-\mathbb{E}\left[\theta(X)\right])^{r}

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\sum_{r\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\ell(x)P(x)\theta(x)(1-\mathbb{E}\left[\theta(X)\right])^{r}

\displaystyle\hskip 36.98866pt+\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\sum_{r\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}r\ell(\phi)P(x)\theta(x)(1-\mathbb{E}\left[\theta(X)\right])^{r}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\ell(x)P(x)\theta(x)}{\mathbb{E}\left[\theta(X)\right]}+\frac{\ell(\phi)(1-\mathbb{E}\left[\theta(X)\right])}{\mathbb{E}\left[\theta(X)\right]}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[L(\theta)\right]}{\mathbb{E}\left[\theta(X)\right]}.

For
\mathbb{E}\left[R_{2}\right]
, it follows from (
1
) that

\mathbb{E}\left[R_{2}\right]=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{2}^{2}\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{2}Z_{1}\right]-\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{2}\right],

since
\mathbb{E}\left[Z_{2}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[Z_{1}\right]
. Also, note that
Z_{1}
only depends on the symbol
x\in\mathcal{X}
received at time
S_{1}
which in turn can depend only on the symbols
X_{n}
for
n\leq S_{1}-1
.
On the other hand,
Y_{2}=S_{2}-S_{1}
depends on symbols
X_{n}
for
n\geq S_{1}
and the outputs of the independent coin tosses corresponding to randomization
\theta
.
Therefore,
Z_{1}

is independent of
Y_{2}
, whereby

\mathbb{E}\left[R_{2}\right]=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{2}^{2}\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{2}\right]\left(\mathbb{E}\left[Z_{1}\right]-\frac{1}{2}\right).

Next, note that
Z_{1}
takes the value
\ell(x)
,
x\in\mathcal{X}
, when the
symbol received at
S_{1}
is
x
. This latter event happens with
probability

\sum_{r=0}^{\infty}(1-\mathbb{E}\left[\theta(X)\right])^{r}\theta(x)P(x)=\frac{\theta(x)P(x)}{\mathbb{E}\left[\theta(X)\right]},

and so, by the definition of
L(\theta)
in (
13
),

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[Z_{1}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{x}\ell(x)\theta(x)P(x)}{\mathbb{E}\left[\theta(X)\right]}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[L(\theta)\right]}{\mathbb{E}\left[\theta(X)\right]}-\frac{\ell(\emptyset)(1-\mathbb{E}\left[\theta(X)\right])}{\mathbb{E}\left[\theta(X)\right]}.

Then by denoting
p_{\emptyset}=1-\mathbb{E}\left[\theta(X)\right]
, the second moment
\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{1}^{2}\right]
can be computed by observing the following recursion:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{1}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\sum_{r\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}(\ell(x)+r\ell(\emptyset))^{2}P(x)\theta(x)p_{\emptyset}^{r}

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\ell(x)^{2}P(x)\theta(x)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+p_{\emptyset}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\sum_{r\in\mathbb{N}}(\ell(x)+r\ell(\emptyset))^{2}P(x)\theta(x)p_{\emptyset}^{r-1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\ell(x)^{2}P(x)\theta(x)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+p_{\emptyset}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\sum_{r\in\mathbb{N}}\big(\ell(x)+(r-1)\ell(\emptyset)\big)^{2}P(x)\theta(x)p_{\emptyset}^{r-1}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+2\ell(\emptyset)p_{\emptyset}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\sum_{r\in\mathbb{N}}\big(\ell(x)+(r-1)\ell(\emptyset)\big)P(x)\theta(x)p_{\emptyset}^{r-1}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+p_{\emptyset}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\sum_{r\in\mathbb{N}}\ell(\emptyset)^{2}P(x)\theta(x)p_{\emptyset}^{r-1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\ell(x)^{2}P(x)\theta(x)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+p_{\emptyset}\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{1}^{2}\right]+2\ell(\emptyset)(1-\mathbb{E}\left[\theta(X)\right])\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{1}\right]+\ell(\emptyset)^{2}p_{\emptyset},

which upon rearrangement yields

\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{1}^{2}\right]=\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[L(\theta)^{2}\right]}{\mathbb{E}\left[\theta(X)\right]}+2\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{1}\right]\cdot\frac{\ell(\emptyset)p_{\emptyset}}{\mathbb{E}\left[\theta(X)\right]}.

Upon combining the relations derived above, we get

\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[R_{2}\right]}{\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{1}\right]}=\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[L(\theta)^{2}\right]}{2\mathbb{E}\left[L(\theta)\right]}+\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[L(\theta)\right]}{\mathbb{E}\left[\theta(X)\right]}-\frac{1}{2},

which with (
20
) completes the proof.

It remains to establish (
20
). The proof is a simple
extension of the renewal reward theorem to our sequence of rewards

R_{n}
in which adjacent terms may be dependent. We include it here for
completeness. Note that
(Y_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a sequence of non-negative iid random variables with mean

\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{1}\right]
, and
S_{n}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}Y_{k}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. The sequence

\{S_{n}\}
serves as a sequence of renewal times and
R_{n}
denotes the reward accumulated in the
n
th
renewal interval (though not in the
standard iid sense). Define

N(t)
to be the number of receptions up to time
t>0
,
i.e.
,

\displaystyle N(t)=\sup{\{n:S_{n}\leq t\}},

and
R(t)
to be the cumulative reward accumulated till time
t
,

i.e.
,

\displaystyle R(t)=\sum_{k=1}^{N(t)}R_{k}.

With this notation, we have

\displaystyle\frac{R(t)}{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{k=1}^{N(t)}R_{k}}{t}

(21)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{k=1}^{N(t)}R_{k}}{N(t)}.\frac{N(t)}{t}.

(22)

Note that

\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{k=1}^{\lfloor\frac{N(t)}{2}\rfloor}\sum_{i\in\{0,1\}}R_{2k+i}}{N(t)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\sum_{k=2}^{N(t)}R_{k}}{N(t)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\sum_{k=1}^{\left\lceil\frac{N(t)}{2}\right\rceil}\sum_{i\in\{0,1\}}R_{2k+i}}{N(t)}.

We now analyze the two bounds in the previous set of inequalities.
Since
\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{1}\right]
is finite, we get (see
[
81
]
for a
proof)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{N(t)}{t}\to\frac{1}{\mathbb{E}\left[Y_{1}\right]}\quad a.s.,

(23)

which also shows that
N(t)\to\infty\quad a.s.
as
t\to\infty
.
Therefore, for
i\in\{0,1\}
,

\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{k=1}^{\left\lceil\frac{N(t)}{2}\right\rceil}R_{2k+i}}{N(t)}=\frac{\sum_{k=1}^{\left\lceil\frac{N(t)}{2}\right\rceil}R_{2k+i}}{\left\lceil\frac{N(t)}{2}\right\rceil}\cdot\frac{\left\lceil\frac{N(t)}{2}\right\rceil}{N(t)}.

Since
(R_{2k+i})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is iid and
N(t)\to\infty\quad a.s.
as
t\to\infty
, strong law of large
numbers yields

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{\sum_{k=1}^{\left\lceil\frac{N(t)}{2}\right\rceil}R_{2k+i}}{\left\lceil\frac{N(t)}{2}\right\rceil}=\mathbb{E}\left[R_{2}\right]\quad a.s.\quad\forall i\in\{0,1\},

which further gives

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{\sum_{k=1}^{\left\lceil\frac{N(t)}{2}\right\rceil}\sum_{i\in\{0,1\}}R_{2k+i}}{N(t)}=\mathbb{E}\left[R_{2}\right]\quad a.s..

Similarly,

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{\sum_{k=1}^{\lfloor\frac{N(t)}{2}\rfloor}\sum_{i\in\{0,1\}}R_{2k+i}}{N(t)}=\mathbb{E}\left[R_{2}\right]\quad a.s..

Combining the observations above, we get

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{\sum_{k=1}^{N(t)}R_{k}}{N(t)}=\mathbb{E}\left[R_{2}\right]\quad a.s.,

which together with (
22
) and (
23
) yields (
20
).

2
Proof of Theorem
5.1

Our proof is based on noticing that the minmax cost
\Delta^{*}(P)
in
(
8
)
involves weighted average length with weights
g_{z,Q,P}(x)
. In
fact, we will see below that there is no loss in restricting to
nonnegative weights, whereby our cost has a form of average length
with respect to a distribution that depends on
(z,Q)
.
The broad idea of the proof is to establish that a optimal code corresponding
to the
least favorable
choice of
(z,Q)
is minmax
optimal. However, the proof is technical since our cost function may not satisfy the assumptions in a standard
saddle-point theorem.

A simpler form of the minmax cost
\Delta^{*}(P)
from (
6
) is given by

\displaystyle{\Delta^{*}(P)=\min_{\ell\in\Lambda}\max_{z\geq 0}f(\ell,z),}

(24)

where

\displaystyle f(\ell,z):=-z^{2}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[L\right]}{2}+z\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\left[L^{2}\right]}+\mathbb{E}\left[L\right].

(25)

We seek to apply the following version of Sion’s minmax theorem to the function
f
.

Theorem 8.1

(Sion’s Minmax Theorem
[
84
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be convex space and
\mathcal{Y}
be a convex, compact space. Let
h
be a function on
\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}
which is convex on
\mathcal{X}
for every fixed
y
in
\mathcal{Y}

and concave on
\mathcal{Y}
for every fixed
x
in
\mathcal{X}
. Then,

\inf_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\sup_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}h(x,y)=\sup_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}\inf_{x\in\mathcal{X}}h(x,y).

Indeed, the following lemma shows that our function
f
satisfies the convexity requirements of Sion’s minmax theorem.

Lemma 8.2
.

f(\ell,z)
is convex in
\ell
for every fixed
z\geq 0

and concave in
z
for a fixed
\ell\in\Lambda
.

Proof.

To show that
f(\ell,z)
is a convex function of
\ell
for every
fixed
z\geq 0
, it suffices to show that
\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\left[L^{2}\right]}
is
convex in
L=\ell(X)
. To that end, let
L_{1}=\ell_{1}(X)
and

L_{2}=\ell_{2}(X)
, for some
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
in
\lambda
. For all

\lambda\in[0,1]
,

\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\lambda L_{1}+(1-\lambda)L_{2}\right)^{2}\right]}\leq\lambda\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\left[L_{1}^{2}\right]}+(1-\lambda)\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\left[L_{2}^{2}\right]},

where the inequality is by Minkowski inequality for
\|L\|_{2}
.

The concavity in
z
can be seen easily by noticing that
\frac{\partial^{2}f(\ell,z)}{\partial z^{2}}\leq 0
for all
\ell
in
\lambda
.
∎

However, our underlying domains of optimization are not compact. Our proof below circumvents this difficulty by showing that we may replace one of the domains by a compact set. For ease of reading, we divide the proof into 3 steps; we begin by
summarize the flow at a high-level. The first step is to show that this minmax cost remains unchanged
when the domain of
z
is restricted to a bounded interval
[0,K]
for
a sufficiently large
K
.
This will allow us to interchange
\min_{l\in\Lambda}
and
\max_{z\in[0,K]}
in the second step by using Theorem
8.1
to obtain

\displaystyle\Delta^{*}(P)=\max_{z\in[0,K]}\min_{\ell\in\Lambda}f(\ell,z).

(26)

Furthermore, we then use Corollary
4.2
to linearize the cost. But this brings in the maximization over an additional parameter
Q
,
which we again interchange with the minimum over
\ell
using Sion’s minmax theorem (Theorem
8.1
). Note that the required convexity of the cost function is easy to see; we note it in the following lemma.

Lemma 8.3
.

For every fixed
z\geq 0
,
\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}g_{z,Q,P}(x)\ell(x)
is convex in
\ell
for a fixed
Q\ll P

and concave in
Q
for a fixed
\ell\in\Lambda
.

Proof.

For every fixed
z\geq 0
, the cost function
\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}g_{z,Q,P}(x)\ell(x)
is linear, and thereby convex,
in
\ell
for a fixed
Q
. For concavity in
Q
, note that
for a fixed
\ell\in\Lambda
, the function
\sqrt{Q(x)}
is a concave function of
Q(x)
, for all
x
in
\mathcal{X}
.
∎

Thus, we obtain

\Delta^{*}(P)=\max_{z\in[0,K],Q\ll P}\min_{\ell\in\Lambda}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}g_{z,Q,P}(x)\ell(x).

In the final step, we will establish that the optimal code
for linear cost with weights corresponding to the least favorable
(z,Q)
is minmax optimal.
We now present each step in detail.

Step 1

We begin by noting that there is no loss in
restricting to codes with
11
11

11

For simplicity, we assume that

\log\mathcal{X}
is an integer.

\mathbb{E}\left[L\right]\leq\log{|\mathcal{X}|}
. Indeed,
note that for
\mathbb{E}\left[L\right]>\log{|\mathcal{X}|}
the average age is bounded as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[L\right]+\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[L^{2}\right]}{2\mathbb{E}\left[L\right]}\geq\frac{3}{2}\mathbb{E}\left[L\right]>\frac{3}{2}\log{|\mathcal{X}|},

(27)

where we have used Jensen’s inequality.
On the other hand, a
fixed-length code with
\ell(x)=\log|\mathcal{X}|
attains

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[L\right]+\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[L^{2}\right]}{2\mathbb{E}\left[L\right]}=\frac{3}{2}\log{|\mathcal{X}|},

(28)

which gives the desired form

\displaystyle\Delta^{*}(P)

\displaystyle=\min_{\ell\in\Lambda,\mathbb{E}\left[L\right]\leq\log{\mathcal{X}}}\mathbb{E}\left[L\right]+\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[L^{2}\right]}{2\mathbb{E}\left[L\right]}

\displaystyle=\min_{\ell\in\Lambda,\mathbb{E}\left[L\right]\leq\log{\mathcal{X}}}\max_{z\in\mathbb{R}}f(\ell,z),

(29)

where
f(\ell,z)
is defined in (
25
).
Also, for a fixed
\ell
in
\Lambda
the function
f(\ell,z)
attains its maximum at

z^{*}(\ell)
given by

z^{*}(\ell):=\frac{\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\left[L^{2}\right]}}{\mathbb{E}\left[L\right]}.

For
\mathbb{E}\left[L\right]\leq\log|\mathcal{X}|
, the maximizer
z^{*}(\ell)
is bounded
as
12
12

12

We assume without loss of generality that
P(x)>0
for every
x\in\mathcal{X}
.

\displaystyle z^{*}(\ell)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\left[L^{2}\right]}}{H(X)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{\sum_{x}P(x)\ell(x)^{2}}}{H(X)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[L\right]}{H(X)}\sqrt{\max_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\frac{1}{P(x)}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\log|\mathcal{X}|}{H(X)}\sqrt{\frac{1}{\min_{x\in\mathcal{X}}P(x)}},

where the first inequality uses
\mathbb{E}\left[L\right]\geq H(X)
, which holds for
every prefix-free code, and the second holds since
\|a\|_{2}\leq\|a\|_{1}
for any sequence
a=(a_{1},...,a_{n})
.
Denoting

K:=\frac{\log|\mathcal{X}|}{H(X)}\sqrt{\frac{1}{\min_{x\in\mathcal{X}}P(x)}},

(
29
) yields

\displaystyle\Delta^{*}(P)

\displaystyle=\min_{\ell\in\Lambda,\mathbb{E}\left[L\right]\leq\log{|\mathcal{X}|}}\max_{z\in[0,K]}f(\ell,z).

Next, we show that the minmax cost above remains unchanged when we
drop the constraint
\mathbb{E}\left[L\right]\leq\log|\mathcal{X}|
in the outer
minimum,
which will complete the first step
of the proof and establish (
26
).
Indeed, since by (
28
) the minimum over
\ell\in\Lambda
such that
\mathbb{E}\left[L\right]\leq\log|\mathcal{X}|
is at most
(3/2)\log|\mathcal{X}|
, it suffices to show that

\displaystyle\min_{\ell\in\Lambda,\mathbb{E}\left[L\right]>\log|\mathcal{X}|}\max_{z\in[0,K]}f(\ell,z)>\frac{3}{2}\log|\mathcal{X}|.

(30)

We divide the proof of this fact into two cases.
First consider the case when
\ell
in
\Lambda
is such that
\mathbb{E}\left[L\right]>\log|\mathcal{X}|
and
K\geq z^{*}(\ell)
. Then,
\max_{z\in[0,K]}f(\ell,z)
equals
\max_{z\geq 0}f(\ell,z)
, which is bounded below by
(3/2)\log|\mathcal{X}|
using
(
27
) and the definition of
f(\ell,z)
.
For the second case when
\mathbb{E}\left[L\right]>\log|\mathcal{X}|
and
K<z^{*}(\ell)
, we
have

\displaystyle\max_{z\in[0,K]}f(\ell,z)

\displaystyle=-K^{2}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[L\right]}{2}+K\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\left[L^{2}\right]}+\mathbb{E}\left[L\right]

\displaystyle>K^{2}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[L\right]}{2}+\mathbb{E}\left[L\right]

\displaystyle>\frac{3}{2}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[L\right]

\displaystyle>\frac{3}{2}\cdot\log|\mathcal{X}|,

where the first inequality uses
K<z^{*}(\ell)=\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\left[L^{2}\right]}/{\mathbb{E}\left[L\right]}

and the second holds since
K\geq 1
from its definition. Therefore, we have established (
30
), and so we have

\displaystyle\Delta^{*}(P)=\min_{\ell\in\Lambda,\mathbb{E}\left[L\right]\leq\log{|\mathcal{X}|}}\max_{z\in[0,K]}f(\ell,z)=\min_{\ell\in\Lambda}\max_{z\in[0,K]}f(\ell,z).

Step 2

By lemma
8.2
,
f(\ell,z)
is convex in
\ell
for every fixed
z\geq 0

and concave in
z
for a fixed
\ell\in\Lambda
,
z
takes values in a convex compact set
[0,K]
, and
the set
\{\ell:\ell\in\Lambda\}
is convex, we get from Sion’s minmax theorem (Theorem
8.1
) that

\displaystyle\Delta^{*}(P)=\min_{\ell\in\Lambda}\max_{z\in[0,K]}f(\ell,z)=\max_{z\in[0,K]}\min_{\ell\in\Lambda}f(\ell,z).

Using Corollary
4.2
, we have

\displaystyle\|L\|_{2}=\max_{Q\ll P}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}Q(x)^{\frac{1}{2}}P(x)^{\frac{1}{2}}\ell(x),

which by the definition of
f
in (
25
) further yields

\displaystyle{f(\ell,z)=\max_{Q\ll P}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}g_{z,Q,P}(x)\ell(x),}

(31)

where

\displaystyle g_{z,Q,P}(x)=\left(1-\frac{z^{2}}{2}\right)P(x)+z\sqrt{Q(x)P(x)}.

We have obtained

\displaystyle\Delta^{*}(P)=\max_{z\in[0,K]}\min_{\ell\in\Lambda}\max_{Q\ll P}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}g_{z,Q,P}(x)\ell(x).

(32)

From Lemma
8.3
,
\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}g_{z,Q,P}(x)\ell(x)
is convex in
\ell
, for all
Q\ll P
,
and concave in
Q
, for a fixed
\ell\in\Lambda
.
Furthermore, since the set
\{Q:Q\ll P\}
is convex compact for a pmf
P
on finite alphabet, using Sion’s minmax
theorem (Theorem
8.1
) once again, we get

\displaystyle\Delta^{*}(P)=\max_{z\in[0,K]}\max_{Q\ll P}\min_{\ell\in\Lambda}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}g_{z,Q,P}(x)\ell(x),

(33)

which completes our second step.

Step 3

By (
33
), we get

\displaystyle\Delta^{*}(P)\leq\max_{z\geq 0}\max_{Q\ll P}\min_{\ell\in\Lambda}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}g_{z,Q,P}(x)\ell(x).

On the other hand, by (
24
) and (
31
) we have

\displaystyle\Delta^{*}(P)

\displaystyle=\min_{\ell\in\Lambda}\max_{z\geq 0}\max_{Q\ll P}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}g_{z,Q,P}(x)\ell(x)

\displaystyle\geq\max_{z\geq 0}\max_{Q\ll P}\min_{\ell\in\Lambda}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}g_{z,Q,P}(x)\ell(x),

whereby

\displaystyle\Delta^{*}(P)

\displaystyle=\min_{\ell\in\Lambda}\max_{z\geq 0}\max_{Q\ll P}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}g_{z,Q,P}(x)\ell(x)

\displaystyle=\max_{z\geq 0}\max_{Q\ll P}\min_{\ell\in\Lambda}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}g_{z,Q,P}(x)\ell(x),

(34)

which proves the first part of theorem
5.1
.

Next, we claim that in the maxmin formula above, the maximum is attained by a
(z,Q)
for which
g_{z,Q,P}(x)
is non-negative for every
x
. Indeed, if for some
z,Q
there exists an
x^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{X}
such that
g_{z,Q,P}(x^{\prime})
is negative, then the cost
\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}g_{z,Q,P}(x)\ell(x)
is minimized by any
\ell
such that
\ell(x^{\prime})=\infty
and the minimum value is
-\infty
. Such
z,Q
clearly can’t be the optimizer of the maxmin problem,
since for
z=0
, we have
g_{z,Q,P}\geq 0
, which in turn leads to
\min_{\ell\in\Lambda}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}g_{z,Q,P}(x)\ell(x)\geq 0
.

Finally, consider
(z,Q)
such that
g_{z,Q,P}(x)\geq 0
for all
x\in\mathcal{X}
. For such a
(z,Q)
, we seek to identify the minimized
\ell
below:

\displaystyle\min_{\ell\in\Lambda}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}g_{z,Q,P}(x)\ell(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}}g_{z,Q,P}(x^{\prime})\min_{\ell\in\Lambda}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\frac{g_{z,Q,P}(x)}{\sum_{x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}}g_{z,Q,P}(x^{\prime})}\ell(x).

(35)

Thus, our optimization problem reduces to the standard problem of designing minimum average length prefix-free codes for the pmf

P_{z,Q}(x)=\frac{g_{z,Q,P}(x)}{\sum_{x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}}g_{z,Q,P}(x^{\prime})}.

By Shannon’s source coding theorem for variable length codes, the minimum is achieved by

\ell^{*}_{z,Q}(x):=\log{{\frac{\sum_{x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}}g_{z,Q,P}(x)}{g_{z,Q,P}(x)}.}}

Furthermore,
\ell^{*}_{z,Q}
is the unique minimizer in
\Lambda
.

Consider now a maximizer
(z^{*},Q^{*})
of the maxmin problem in (
34
), and let
\ell^{o}=\ell^{*}_{z^{*},Q^{*}}
.
Then, by Lemma
8.5
in the appendix,
(\ell^{o},(z^{*},Q^{*}))
is a saddle-point for the minmax problem in (
34
).
Moreover,
\ell^{o}
is the unique minmax optimal solution.

3
Proof of Theorem
7.4

Denoting

\displaystyle f(\ell,z)=-z^{2}\frac{(L_{\tt th}-\mathbb{E}\left[L\right])}{2}+z\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\left[L^{2}\right]}+\mathbb{E}\left[L\right],

(36)

the optimal cost
\Delta^{*}(P)
can be written as

\displaystyle\Delta^{*}(P)

\displaystyle=\inf_{\ell\in\Lambda,\mathbb{E}\left[L\right]<L_{\tt th}}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[L^{2}\right]}{2(L_{\tt th}-\mathbb{E}\left[L\right])}+\mathbb{E}\left[L\right]

\displaystyle=\min_{\ell\in\Lambda,\mathbb{E}\left[L\right]<L_{\tt th}}\max_{z\geq 0}f(\ell,z).

This form is similar to the one we had in Theorem
5.1
. But the proof there does not extend to the case at hand.
Specifically, note that for each
\ell
,
f(\ell,z)
attains its maximum value for
z^{*}(\ell)=\frac{\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\left[L^{2}\right]}}{(L_{\tt th}-\mathbb{E}\left[L\right])}
which, unlike the quantity that we obtained in the proof of Theorem
5.1
, is unbounded over the set of
\ell\in\Lambda
such that
\mathbb{E}\left[L\right]\leq L_{\tt th}
. However, under the additional assumption
H(X)+\log(1+1/\sqrt{2})<L_{\tt th}
, we can provide a simpler alternative proof.
We rely on the following lemma.

Lemma 8.4
.

Consider a function
h:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\to\mathbb{R}
such that the set
\mathcal{X}
is compact convex, the set
\mathcal{Y}
is convex,
h(x,y)
is a convex function of
x
for every fixed
y
and a concave function of
y
for every fixed
x
. Suppose additionally that there exist a convex subset
\mathcal{X}_{0}
of
\mathcal{X}
and a compact convex subset
\mathcal{Y}_{0}
of
\mathcal{Y}
such that

1.

for every for every
x\in\mathcal{X}_{0}
, an optimizer
y^{*}(x)\in\arg\max_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}h(x,y)
belongs to
\mathcal{Y}_{0}
; and

2.

for every
y\in\mathcal{Y}_{0}
, an optimizer
x^{*}(y)\in\arg\min_{x\in\mathcal{X}}h(x,y)
belongs to
\mathcal{X}_{0}
.

Then,

\min_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\max_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}h(x,y)=\max_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}\min_{x\in\mathcal{X}}h(x,y).

Proof.

Note that since for
x
in
\mathcal{X}_{0}
, the
y
that maximizes
h(x,y)
over
\mathcal{Y}
is in
\mathcal{Y}_{0}
, we get

\min_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\max_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}h(x,y)\leq\min_{x\in\mathcal{X}_{0}}\max_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}h(x,y)=\min_{x\in\mathcal{X}_{0}}\max_{y\in\mathcal{Y}_{0}}h(x,y).

Further, by Sion’s minmax theorem (Theorem
8.1
), the right-side equals

\max_{y\in\mathcal{Y}_{0}}\min_{x\in\mathcal{X}_{0}}h(x,y)
. But by our second assumption, the restriction
x\in\mathcal{X}_{0}
can be dropped, and we have

\max_{y\in\mathcal{Y}_{0}}\min_{x\in\mathcal{X}_{0}}h(x,y)=\max_{y\in\mathcal{Y}_{0}}\min_{x\in\mathcal{X}}h(x,y)\leq\max_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}\min_{x\in\mathcal{X}}h(x,y).

Thus, we have shown
\min_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\max_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}h(x,y)\leq\max_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}\min_{x\in\mathcal{X}}h(x,y)
, which completes the proof since the inequality in the other direction holds as well.
∎

For our minmax cost, we will verify that both the conditions of the lemma above hold under the assumption
H(X)+\log(1+1/\sqrt{2})<L_{\tt th}
. Indeed, first note that for any fixed
\ell\in\Lambda
with
\mathbb{E}\left[L\right]\leq H(X)+\log(1+1/\sqrt{2})
, the maximizer
z
of
f(\ell,z)
given by
\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\left[L^{2}\right]}/(L_{\tt th}-\mathbb{E}\left[L\right])
satisfies

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\left[L^{2}\right]}}{L_{\tt th}-\mathbb{E}\left[L\right]}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{1}{\min_{x}P(x)}}\cdot\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[L\right]}{L_{\tt th}-\mathbb{E}\left[L\right]}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{1}{\min_{x}P(x)}}\cdot\frac{H(X)+\log(1+1/\sqrt{2})}{L_{\tt th}-H(X)-\log(1+1/\sqrt{2})}.

Denote the right-side above by
K
and
L_{\tt th}^{\prime}=H(X)+\log(1+1/\sqrt{2})
.
Therefore, with the set
\{\ell\in\Lambda,\mathbb{E}\left[L\right]\leq L_{\tt th}^{\prime}\}
in the role of
\mathcal{X}_{0}
in Lemma
8.4
,
the set
[0,K]
can play the role of
\mathcal{Y}_{0}
.

To apply Lemma
8.4
, we require two
conditions to hold: first, that

f(l,z)
is a convex function of
\ell
for every fixed
z
and a concave function of
z
for every fixed
\ell
, second, that
for every
z\in[0,K]
, the minimizing
\ell

satisfies
\mathbb{E}\left[L\right]\leq L_{\tt th}^{\prime}
.The first easily follows from (
36
). The proof of this fact is exactly the same as Lemma
8.2
. However, while the second condition can be
shown to be true, the proof of
this fact is almost the same as the proof of our theorem. For
simplicity of presentation, we instead present an alternative proof of
the theorem that uses a slight extension of the lemma above.
Note that from our foregoing discussion and following the proof of the
lemma,
we already have obtained

\Delta^{*}(P)\leq\max_{z\in[0,K]}\min_{\ell\in\Lambda,\mathbb{E}\left[L\right]\leq{L^{\prime}}_{\tt th}}f(\ell,z).

By using Corollary
4.2
and using Sion’s minmax theorem once again, we get

\displaystyle\Delta^{*}(P)

\displaystyle\leq\max_{z\in[0,K]}\max_{Q\ll P}\min_{\ell\in\Lambda,\mathbb{E}\left[L\right]\leq{L^{\prime}}_{\tt th}}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}g_{z,Q,P}(x)\ell(x)-\frac{z^{2}}{2}L_{\tt th},

where

\displaystyle g_{z,Q,P}(x):=\left(1+\frac{z^{2}}{2}\right)P(x)+z\sqrt{Q(x)P(x)}.

In the preceding argument, we can use Sion’s minmax theorem as the following two conditions hold. First, for every fixed
z\geq 0
, the function
\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}g_{z,Q,P}(x)\ell(x)-\frac{z^{2}}{2}L_{\tt th}
is concave in
Q
for a fixed
\ell\in\Lambda
and convex in
\ell
for a fixed
Q\ll P
. Second, the sets
\{Q:Q\ll P\}
and
\{\ell\in\Lambda:\mathbb{E}\left[L\right]\leq{L^{\prime}}_{\tt th}\}
are compact and convex. Proof of the first is exactly the same as that of
8.3
. Second is true as we have restricted to a finite alphabet
\mathcal{X}
.
Thus, we can proceed as in the proof of the lemma, but we need to show now that for every
z\in[0,K]
and
Q\ll P
, the optimal
\ell^{*}(z,Q)
satisfies
\mathbb{E}\left[L^{*}\right]\leq L_{\tt th}^{\prime}

.
Indeed, consider the following optimization problem for a fixed
z
,
Q
:

\displaystyle\min_{\ell\in\Lambda}

\displaystyle\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}g_{z,Q,P}(x)\ell(x)

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}}{{g_{z,Q,P}(x^{\prime})}}\right)\min_{\ell\in\Lambda}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\frac{g_{z,Q,P}(x)}{\sum_{x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}}g_{z,Q,P}(x^{\prime})}\ell(x).

Since
\frac{g_{z,Q,P}(x)}{\sum_{x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}}g_{z,Q,P}(x^{\prime})}
are nonnegative and add to
1
,
in the optimization problem above, we are minimizing the expected prefix
free lengths for a finite alphabet for a particular distribution. Thus,
by Shannon’s Source Coding Theorem, the optimal
\ell^{*}_{z,Q}
is given by

\displaystyle\ell^{*}_{z,Q}(x):=\log\frac{\sum_{x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}}g_{z,Q,P}(x^{\prime})}{g_{z,Q,P}(x)};

in fact, this optimizer is unique. But then for every
x
in
\mathcal{X}
,

\displaystyle\ell^{*}_{z,Q}(x)

\displaystyle=\log\frac{\sum_{x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}}g_{z,Q,P}(x^{\prime})}{g_{z,Q,P(x)}}

\displaystyle=\log\frac{\sum_{x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}}\left(1+\frac{z^{2}}{2}\right)P(x)+\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}z\sqrt{Q(x)P(x)}}{\left(1+\frac{z^{2}}{2}\right)P(x)+z\sqrt{Q(x)P(x)}}

\displaystyle\leq\log\frac{1}{P(x)}

\displaystyle~+\log\left(\frac{\left(1+\frac{z^{2}}{2}\right)}{\left(1+\frac{z^{2}}{2}\right)+z\sqrt{\frac{Q(x)}{P(x)}}}+\frac{z}{\left(1+\frac{z^{2}}{2}\right)+z\sqrt{\frac{Q(x)}{P(x)}}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\log\frac{1}{P(x)}+\log\left(\frac{\left(1+\frac{z^{2}}{2}\right)}{\left(1+\frac{z^{2}}{2}\right)}+\frac{z}{\left(1+\frac{z^{2}}{2}\right)}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\log\frac{1}{P(x)}+\log\left(1+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right),

where the first inequality is by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, the second inequality follows upon noting that
\frac{Q(x)}{P(x)}
is nonnegative, and the last inequality follows from the fact that
z^{2}/2+1\geq\sqrt{2}z
(which holds with equality at
z=\sqrt{2}
).
Thus as a consequence of this inequality the expected code length of
such a code is upper bounded as
follows,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[L^{*}_{z,Q}\right]\leq H(x)+\log\left(1+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right),

(37)

which in the manner of Lemma
8.4
gives

\displaystyle\Delta^{*}(P)=\max_{z\geq 0}\max_{Q\ll P}\min_{\ell\in\Lambda,\mathbb{E}\left[L\right]\leq{L}_{\tt th}}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}g_{z,Q,P}(x)\ell(x)-\frac{z^{2}}{2}L_{\tt th}.

Finally, it remains to establish that
\ell^{*}_{z^{*},Q^{*}}
is the unique minmax optimal solution. This can be shown in exactly the same manner as it was shown for Theorem
5.1
in the previous section; we skip the details.∎

4
A saddle-point lemma

The following simple result is needed to establish the minmax
optimality of our scheme. The first part of the result claims that any
pair of minmax optimal
x
and maxmin optimal
y
forms a saddle
point, a well-known fact. The second part claims that if the minimizer
for the maxmin optimal
y
is unique, then it must also be minmax
optimal and thereby constitute a saddle-point with
y
.

Lemma 8.5
.

Consider the minmax problem
\displaystyle{\min_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\max_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}h(x,y)}
, and
assume that

\min_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\max_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}h(x,y)=\max_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}\min_{x\in\mathcal{X}}h(x,y).

Then, for every pair
(x^{*},y^{*})
such that
\displaystyle{x^{*}\in\arg\min_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\max_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}h(x,y)}
and
\displaystyle{y^{*}\in\arg\max_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}\min_{x\in\mathcal{X}}h(x,y)}

constitutes a saddle-point. Furthermore, if the minimizer
x^{o}(y^{*})

of
\min_{x\in\mathcal{X}}h(x,y^{*})
is unique, then
x^{*}=x^{o}(y^{*})

is the unique minmax optimal solution.

Proof.

Since minmax and maxmin costs are assumed to be equal, by the definition of
x^{*}
and

y^{*}
, we have

\displaystyle h(x,y^{*})

\displaystyle\geq\max_{y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Y}}\min_{x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}}h(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\min_{x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}}\max_{y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Y}}h(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\geq h(x^{*},y),

(38)

for all
x
in
\mathcal{X}
and
y
in
\mathcal{Y}
.
Upon substituting
x^{*}
for
x
and
y^{*}
for
y
, we get that
x^{*}

is a minimizer of
h(x,y^{*})
and
y^{*}
a maximizer of
h(x^{*},y)
. Therefore,
(x^{*},y^{*})
forms a saddle-point and
h(x^{*},y^{*})=\min_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\max_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}h(x,y)
.

Turning now to the second part, suppose that
x^{\prime}
, too, is minmax optimal.
Then, using (
38
) with
x=x^{\prime}
and
y=y^{*}
, we get that
x^{\prime}
must be a minimizer
of
h(x,y^{*})
as well.
But since this minimizer is unique,
x^{\prime}
must coincide with
x^{o}
.

∎

Proof of Lemma
5.2

Denoting

c_{P}(z,Q):=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}g_{z,Q,P}(x)\log\frac{\sum_{x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}}g_{z,Q,P}(x^{\prime})}{g_{z,Q,P}(x)},

we begin by observing the
concavity of
c_{P}(z,Q)
. Recall the notations
\mathcal{G}=\{z\geq 0,Q\in\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{X}|}:g_{z,Q,P}(x)\geq 0\quad\forall x\in\mathcal{X}\}

and
g_{z,Q,P}(x)=(1-z^{2}/2)P(x)+z\sqrt{Q(x)P(x)}
.

Lemma 8.6
.

The function
c_{P}(z,Q)
is concave in
Q
for each fixed
z

and is concave in
z
for each fixed
Q
, over the set
\mathcal{G}
.

Proof.

For the first part, (
2
) yields that for every
(z,Q)\in\mathcal{G}
,

\displaystyle\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}g_{z,Q,P}(x)

\displaystyle\log\frac{\sum_{x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}}g_{z,Q,P}(x^{\prime})}{g_{z,Q,P}(x)}

\displaystyle=\min_{\ell\in\Lambda}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}g_{z,Q,P}(x)\ell(x).

Also, for every fixed
z
, the function
g_{z,Q,P}(x)
is concave in
Q
, and thereby
\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}g_{z,Q,P}(x)\ell(x)
, is concave in

Q
. Thus, since the minimum of concave functions is concave,

c_{P}(z,Q)
is concave in
Q
for a fixed
z
. Similarly, we can show
concavity in
z
for a fixed
Q
since
g_{z,Q,P}(x)
is concave in

z
, too, for every fixed
Q
.
∎

We now complete the proof of Lemma
5.2
.
We will show that for any
(z,Q)
which is feasible for optimization
problem (
9
), we can find a feasible

(z,Q^{\prime})
with
Q^{\prime}
satisfying (
11
), and

c_{P}(z,Q)\leq c_{P}(z,Q^{\prime}).

Indeed, consider

Q^{\prime}(x):=Q(A_{i})/|A_{i}|
for all
x\in\mathcal{X}
. The remainder of the proof is divided into two parts, the
first proving the feasibility of
Q^{\prime}
and the second
proving
c_{P}(z,Q)\leq c_{P}(z,Q^{\prime}).

Feasibility of
(z,Q^{\prime})

From the feasibility of

(z,Q)
, for all symbols
x
in
A_{i}
and for all
i
in

[M_{P}]
,
g_{z,Q,P}(x)\geq 0
, whereby

\displaystyle\sum_{x\in A_{i}}g_{z,Q,P}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in A_{i}}\left(1-\frac{{z}^{2}}{2}\right)P(x)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt+{z}\sum_{x\in A_{i}}\sqrt{Q(x)P(x)}

\displaystyle=\left(1-\frac{{z}^{2}}{2}\right)P(A_{i})+{z}\sum_{x\in A_{i}}\sqrt{Q(x)P(x)}

\displaystyle\geq\left(1-\frac{{z}^{2}}{2}\right)P(A_{i})+{z}\sqrt{Q^{\prime}(A_{i})P(A_{i})}

\displaystyle=|A_{i}|g_{z,Q^{\prime},P}(x)

\displaystyle\geq 0,

where the first inequality is by Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, the positivity
of
z
, and the assumption that
P(x)=P(A_{i})/|A_{i}|
for every
x
in

A_{i}
, and the final identity uses definition of
Q^{\prime}
. This
proves the feasibility of
(z,Q^{\prime})
for the optimization
problem (
9
).

Proof of optimality

Denoting by
\Pi(A_{1})
the set of all permutations of the elements of

A_{1}
, let
Q^{\pi}
be the distribution given by

Q^{\pi}(x)=\begin{cases}&Q(\pi(x)),\quad\forall x\in A_{1}\\
&Q(x),\quad\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Then, the distribution
\overline{Q}=(1/|\Pi(A_{1})|)\cdot\sum_{\pi\in\Pi(A_{1})}Q^{\pi}
satisfies

\overline{Q}(x)=\begin{cases}&\frac{1}{|A_{1}|}\cdot Q(A_{1}),\quad\forall x\in A_{1}\\
&Q(x),\quad\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Since by Lemma
8.6

c_{P}(z,Q)
is concave in
Q
for every fixed
z
, we get

c_{P}(z,\overline{Q})\geq\frac{1}{|\Pi(A_{1})|}\cdot\sum_{\pi\in\Pi(A_{1})}c_{P}(z,Q^{\pi}).

Furthermore, note that
g_{z,Q^{\pi},P}(x)=g_{z,Q,P}(\pi(x))

since

P(x)=P(A_{1})/|A_{1}|
for every
x
in
A_{1}
, and thereby

c_{P}(z,Q^{\pi})=c_{P}(z,Q)
for every
\pi\in\Pi(A_{1})
. Therefore,
combining the observations above, we obtain
c_{P}(z,\overline{Q})\geq c_{P}(z,Q)
.

Repeating this argument by iteratively using permutations of
A_{i}
for
i\geq 2
, we obtain the required inequality

c_{P}(z,Q^{\prime})\geq c_{P}(z,Q).

∎

9
Concluding Remarks

In this chapter, we studied the source coding problem where the goal is to minimize

\mathbb{E}\left[L\right]+\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[L^{2}\right]}{2\mathbb{E}\left[L\right]}

subject to the constraint that the code lengths satisfy Kraft’s inequality. We saw that this problem differs from the standard source coding problem where the goal is to minimize
\mathbb{E}\left[L\right],
and the classic source-coding solutions such as deploying Shannon codes may be suboptimal. Our main result was a structural result showing that the optimal code lengths for the relaxed version of the problem are Shannon lengths for tilting of the original distribution. Our recipe to prove this result was to linearize the cost function in terms of length by first expressing as the optimal value of a quadratic maximization problem over a new variable. Then, we use a variational formula for the
L_{2}
norm of a random variable to linearize the cost. We believe that our approach can be used to prove similar structural results for other source coding problems, thereby gaining computational insights into solving them, as we saw with the application of our recipe for the problem of designing source codes with minimum delay.
